Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: Article: Do Bond Investors Get Rewarded for Taking Dur. Risk? (P. 1) Edward F. McQuarrie, William J. Bernstein, 11/6

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

[sorry for the delayed reply. We just finished up Part 2, should appear after the holiday.]
Thank you for your input.
typical.investor, I have to concur with StillGoing: your time period of 2010 - 2025 is too short to have probative value. You need at least a complete bear and bond market cycle. That's why we liked the post-1945, 80-year span: 36 years of bear, 40 years of bull, 4 years of renewed bear.
Agreed. I'm looking at the FRED H.15 data from April 1953 onward next—that's where the monthly series begins, and it also avoids the wartime yield-curve control era prior to the 1951 Fed–Treasury Accord.
In our thinking, the great 40-year bull 1981 - 2021 created the mother of all recency biases: of course bonds give you a coupon plus capital gain--that's just what bonds do! Not.

And of course, a bull market favors duration.
Agreed. It's why the period I looked at was actually rising rates overall.
Next, I don't think you can test a ladder against a constant maturity approach by switching from the Coleman / Ibbotson yield curve (which we used) to the Treasury HR 15 curve (which Shiller uses). I'd recommend actual purchase prices. These are available from the CRSP Treasury file--I access it through my university library subscription to WRDS.

That may be too heavy a lift--not everybody has access to a University library. But to go beyond a thoughtful contribution to the forum, to something you could publish, I think that's the threshold: buy the bonds at the ask, hold, handle the semi-annual coupon payments.
I don't intend to test further against constant maturity, but will instead look at a hold-to-maturity ladder vs a fund or sector (like 7-10y) using the same Treasury H.15 curve.

As an investor, I realistically do have a choice of using a ladder or fund. Constant-maturity methodology isn't something that really happens in the normal course of investing I think.
Next, remember that the 5-year maturity was not a mountain peak--it was the point of rapid flattening as far as return by maturity is concerned. Calculating a return on a 10-year ladder, with maturity of 5.5 years, and showing a dozen basis points of extra return, relative to exactly 5 years, does not move the ball down the field.
Viewing a 10-year ladder as simply "5.5 years of maturity" misses an essential return mechanism of individual bonds: pull-to-par.

A constant-maturity series buys a bond, sells it a month later, and buys back the original maturity. In a rising-rate regime, that forces repeated capital losses; in a falling-rate regime, it harvests continuous capital gains. Buying a bond and holding it to maturity doesn't work that way—your return is anchored by your starting yield and pull-to-par, with rate changes only affecting coupon reinvestment.

You can see how different those two mechanisms are in the 1953–2026 H.15 data (762 rolling 10-year windows). Let's strip out the shared starting yield and look at the correlation.

Correlation of deviations from starting yield (Realized 10y CAGR − Starting Yield):
10y CMT vs. a single 10y bond held to maturity: -0.279
10y CMT vs. a rolling 0–10y HTM ladder: +0.633

For a single 10-year bond held to maturity, the rate-change risk literally points in the opposite direction of 10y CMT. In the 1953–1981 bear market, 10y CMT lagged a buy-and-hold 10-year Treasury in 100% of rolling 10-year windows (by a median of -102 bps/yr); in the 1981–2020 bull market, it beat it in 100% of windows (by a median of +163 bps/yr).

That's why I think the "coupon plus capital gain" recency bias you noted is more a constant-maturity artifact than how hold-to-maturity Treasuries behave. Of course, I acknowledge that real-world bond funds that don't hold to maturity will also realize capital gains in bull markets and capital losses in bear markets, although likely to a lesser degree than CMT—which is why I'm testing hold-to-maturity ladders against funds.
Last, much of our analysis was focused on stock-bond portfolios, and the question of which Treasury best supplements stocks. The mean-variance charts, per Markowitz, assume regular rebalancing back to the indicated weights.

Ladders do not rebalance. You need Tobin (2-fund separation theorem), not Markowitz, given a ladder. Space constraints prevented us from going there.
I, too, am very much concerned with stock-bond portfolios. Where we differ is that I find it straightforward to rebalance with a rolling ladder inside a 60/40 portfolio:

When stocks drop: Maturing rungs and semiannual coupons provide natural cash flow to buy equities. If a sharp drop requires more than the maturing rung provides, selling the shortest remaining rung is functionally identical to selling a T-bill.

When stocks surge: Trimming equities funds new top rungs at the long end of the ladder.

Any temporary drift in average maturity from selling the front rung or adding to the back rung is largely self-correcting as the remaining rungs age. And because a rolling nominal ladder carries real duration and price risk, it isn't a Tobin risk-free asset—it's a fixed-income sleeve that rebalances Markowitz-style just fine.

I do again thank you for your input and look forward to your next work.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Is withdrawal rate modeling too simplistic to be useful to most people?

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If you were to attempt to forecast your spending in retirement by year, you'll almost certainly have many years with big, non-yearly expenses. If you buy that assertion and if this describes you, how is the notion of a "safe" withdrawal rate useful to you? If you have enough saved that a safe withdrawal rate would support even your lumpy-expense years, then that does seem like a potentially useful gauge. But if you don't and your modeling shows some years with a required withdrawal rate well below whatever you believe to be "safe" but others well above that, are you still using the notion of a SWR? If so, how?
https://tpawplanner.com/ and https://levelspend.github.io/ help with this. You review the plan annually. You can build in/plan for lumpy expenses, etc. It's not a static withdrawal strategy but a dynamic/variable withdrawal strategy as things change. The only constant is change.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Anyone having experience with ARVA [Annually Recalculated Virtual Annuity]?

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

[I don't see best third as a special case of TPAW.
What I meant is that you can get the same results with TPAW if you plug in the right parameters.
TPAW, VPW, ABW, the best third are all riffs on amortization for the withdrawal of the variable portion of the portfolio. The best third is the only one that actively builds in a TIPS floor, when TPAW or VPW allow it and can deal with it but don't make it part of the strategy. That's what I like about it (and something I practiced before I was aware of any of those tools)

When it comes to what AA and discount rate to use, grab your crystal ball and choose your poison.
Understood. Best third is the closest to what we do. We built our own income floors with tipsladder.com. I built an SS TIPS bridge that matches what my SS will be in 5.5 years or so and DW is already on SS. We also include dividends from our taxable stock holdings as part of our daily spending, but not otherwise selling stock for that. Not everybody can put together a detailed plan on their own so having best third is a good thing as an integrated tool.

In terms of discount rate, for stock I use a simple 1/CAPE. If I had bond funds as risky assets, then I'd use the current nominal yield and geometrically subtract expected inflation or I'd use a TIPS fund and just use the real yield directly. Either way you slice it, future bond fund returns are more deterministic than future stock funds returns are, and the net result is there is less "noise" in the withdrawals due to the bond portion of a portfolio when amorting than there is from the stock portion. I'd also probably also consider bond usage whose durations were compatible with my investment horizon at any given time. If you have a long duration stock fund, for example, but only 5 years remaining in your plan then the future return predictions will be really bad vs. realized returns. I believe Ben and team are also working on adding something like this to TPAW.

cheers.

With help from Matt Damon and Zack Snyder, Ford announces return to Le Mans

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The giant in American automaking announced on Thursday, Nov. 26 that it will return to the iconic endurance race held annually in France with its new Ford GT41 Hypercar.

It's been 60 years since Ford became the first American automaker to win at Le Mans, as three Fords — piloted by Ken Miles, Bruce McLaren and Ronnie Bucknum — finished first, second and third at the race. The 1966 24 Hours of Le Mans and the events leading up to it were dramatized in a 2019 Academy Award-winning film Ford v Ferrari, directed by James Mangold and starring Matt Damon and Christian Bale. The film was nominated for Best Picture and won Oscars for Best Film Editing and Best Sound Editing.

To help Ford with the announcement, Damon provided his voice to a narration over a commercial directed by Zack Snyder, known for films such as 300 and Justice League. The commercial aired on Thanksgiving during the NFL game between the Chicago Bears and Ford's hometown Detroit Lions.

The nearly two-minute long commercial was titled "Go Like Hell" — a phrase that Carroll Shelby wrote on a board to show to driver Ken Miles, telling him to push the car past its limits as they raced at the 24 Hours of Daytona ahead of Le Mans.

"The amazing part about getting a chance to work on this 'Go Like Hell' spot for Ford Racing was the chance to mix the nostalgia of the '66 GT40 with this modern Hypercar and its incredible engineering, as well as this narrative that follows the transporting of the car across America," Snyder said in a statement. "It's during this journey that we get to see what drives Ford to win. And as a filmmaker, those elements inspired me to make some cool images."

Damon added in a statement: "Telling the story of Le Mans '66 was one of the highlights of my career, so getting to lend my voice to the next chapter felt very full circle. Revisiting this one with Ford and Zack, as they take on the world's toughest racing all over again, was a real privilege."

The commercial revisits a familiar filming location, as the hangar used to load the GT41's shipping container is the same location featured in Ford v Ferrari as the site of the Ford GT40 race workshop.

Ford won four consecutive Le Mans from 1966 to 1969. Their second win in 1967 was by a team that featured AJ Foyt and Dan Gurney, two of the greatest American racecar drivers of all time. Foyt won four Indy 500s and a Daytona 500, while Gurney had four Formula 1 Grand Prix wins and five career NASCAR Grand National Series wins.

Six decades later, Ford remains the only American automaker to win at Le Mans. Toyota has won six of the last nine 24 Hours of Le Mans, with Ferrari winning the other three.

"Having completed six European tests, culminating in our first 30-hour endurance test at Aragon, we're now preparing for the next phase of development on American soil," said Dan Sayers, the Ford Racing Hypercar program manager. "We're under no illusions about the scale of the challenge ahead. Our focus remains on developing the car's performance and reliability, while continuing to evolve as a team ahead of our competitive debut."

Ford will also enter the GT41 Hypercar into the FIA World Endurance Championship, which begins in Qatar in March. That race is where the GT41 Hypercar will officially make its debut. Ford Racing said in a statement that it "continues to expand its global racing efforts" to 41 different racing series across six continents.

"We're going back to Le Mans next summer in June to take on Ferrari and the best of the world to win Le Mans outright. It's an all-Ford effort. We're not having another company manage the athletes and the effort — this is all on Ford with some key partnerships," Ford CEO Jim Farley told FOX Business. "We really think of ourselves as America's Race Team."

The Gt41 Hypercar takes inspiration from the classic GT40 and stands 41 inches tall. The engine was designed at Ford's home in Dearborn, Michigan, and the naturally aspirated Coyote-based V8 is powered by an advanced hybrid propulsion system. Ford also said it brought its "discipline learned through our partnership with Red Bull Racing" in Formula 1 to accelerate the process.

This article originally appeared on USA TODAY: With help from Matt Damon and Zack Snyder, Ford announces return to Le Mans

Prediction: These 3 Consumer Stocks Will Beat the S&P 500 Over the Next 10 Years

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I think Ferrari, Hilton, and Amer Sports have the brands, the growth runways, and the discipline to outrun the S&P 500 over the next decade.

By Micah Zimmerman – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:30PM EST

Beating the S&P 500 sounds simple until you remember how good the index already is, since it has compounded at about 9.9% a year on a total return basis going back to 1928. So a stock that beats the index over 10 years needs more than a good quarter, it needs a business that can keep growing over the long term.

When I look for that kind of staying power in the consumer world, I aim for three things: a brand that people pay up for, a long expansion runway, and a management team that protects both. Here are three consumer stocks that I think long-term investors should consider for the decade ahead.

Ferrari (RACE +2.96%) deliberately builds fewer cars than people want, and that scarcity is the heart of its economic moat, which is a durable advantage that keeps rivals from stealing its customers.

CEO Benedetto Vigna said in the third-quarter release that deliveries "reflect our clients' ever-deepening desire to make each Ferrari uniquely their own." Those personal touches add profit to every car without adding volume.

Demand isn't the problem. Vigna said on the call, "We continue to experience a healthy demand across all geographies, with an order book that enters 2028."

The risks are a premium valuation, at roughly 36 times earnings, and the shift to electric models like the Luce, which has to win over buyers who love engines.

Hilton (HLT +0.52%) mostly doesn't own its hotels; it collects fees by managing and licensing its brands to owners through a franchise model, which is an arrangement where a business pays to use another company's brand and systems. That lets it grow without spending heavily on buildings.

CEO Chris Nassetta said in the third-quarter release, "We achieved our highest quarter ever for organic openings." He also said on the call, "Globally, Hilton is still less than 6% of supply providing a long runway for continued growth."

Travel is cyclical, though. CFO Kevin Jacobs said, "RevPAR in China declined 5.4% in the quarter," referring to revenue per available room, which is a key hotel measure of how well rooms are filled and priced. Middle East conflict is weighing on that region, too, and the stock trades near 49 times earnings.

Amer Sports (AS +2.56%) owns Arc'teryx jackets, Salomon shoes, and Wilson tennis gear, and every segment grew by double digits in its latest quarter. CEO James Zheng said, "I am very confident in the future outlook for our company"

The U.S. opportunity is what excites me most. Arc'teryx CEO Stuart Haselden noted that the brand has about as many stores in the U.S. as in Canada, which he said "makes it very apparent the potential that we have to expand in the U.S." Management also said Europe's store traffic is a challenge, and higher freight costs are nibbling at margins.

None of these companies will beat the market every year, but I think long-term investors should consider owning all three and giving their growth stories the full decade they deserve. Great brands tend to compound quietly.

What ties these three together is pricing power, which is the ability to raise prices without losing customers, and I think that matters more over a decade than any single quarter's results.

Ferrari's buyers wait years for their cars, Hilton's owners keep signing up for its brands, and Arc'teryx shoppers happily pay premium prices for gear that lasts.

There will be rough stretches, whether it's a travel slowdown, a weak luxury market, or a cold winter for outdoor sales, but investors who hold through those dips give each business time to prove its case. In the end, patience is the edge most investors give up too easily.

When our analyst team has a stock tip, it can pay to listen. After all, Stock Advisor's total average return is 777%* — a market-crushing outperformance compared to 199% for the S&P 500.

FreightCar America: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Management is betting on an upcoming fleet replacement cycle, leaving open the question of how quickly volume returns to support the margin story.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 26, 2026 at 5:27PM EST

10 stocks we like better than FreightCar America ›

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 10, 2026, FreightCar America (RAIL +4.39%) reaffirmed its full-year revenue guidance of $370 million to $410 million despite lower quarterly railcar deliveries. The business isn't relying solely on new railcar manufacturing. Expanding aftermarket sales and internal cost cuts could position the manufacturer for profit growth when railcar demand rebounds. Three things management said stand out.

FreightCar America acquired three aftermarket suppliers within a year, the latest after quarter-end. Organic parts growth and earlier deals boosted third-quarter aftermarket revenue by 65% year over year to $10.8 million with a 44.5% gross margin. This segment generates repeatable revenue that cushions cyclical drops in new railcar orders.

The company expanded its market share in addressable new railcar orders over the trailing 12 months while protecting its pricing discipline. This commercial momentum lifted total backlog value by 28% year over year to $284.5 million.

Management completed a workforce realignment in the second quarter that delivers about $12 million in annualized structural cost savings. The savings reduce fixed overhead without cutting manufacturing capacity. This operational setup could create operating leverage for the anticipated industry replacement cycle.

Railcar storage levels are falling across North America as shippers bring railcars back into service. The manufacturer maintains flexible production lines, expands aftermarket distribution, and reduces structural overhead, aiming to capture margin gains as order volumes rise.

Commercial focus remains tied to pricing discipline and customer retention across major railroads and industrial shippers rather than chasing low-margin volume.

The 5 Greatest Bands of the British Invasion, Ranked

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Throughout the 1960s, there was a huge influx of British rock and pop music into American culture. English bands were dominating the charts and the music scene was becoming more globalized than ever before. The overseas relationship was reciprocal – these British bands were reworking American blues and R&B within a rock format, infusing psychedelic and progressive rock. Concurrently, American rock music began to emulate the British sound. This cultural phenomenon changed the face of music forever.

The British Invasion is an interesting and necessary study in the way music reinvents itself – how every musician is influenced by those who came before. This time period introduced this wonderful merging of sound to an international audience, causing a dizzyingly steep learning curve that produced some of the greatest music of all time. Here are the top five most important bands of the British Invasion.

The Animals formed in 1962 in Newcastle upon Tyne. They drew inspiration from American rhythm and blues artists, getting their start with covers by iconic musicians such as Jimmy Reed and Nina Simone. They had a big hand in the genesis of folk rock with their signature song: a cover of the American folk classic “House of the Rising Sun.” Their version is raw and emotional, capturing the struggles of working-class Americans during this time. The band represented a darker and grittier side of the British Invasion canon, the opposite end of the spectrum from the British pop that was also making its way overseas.

In the latter half of the sixties, they changed their lineup and their name to Eric Burden and The Animals. During this time, they delved into the psychedelic rock space, departing from their earlier blues influences. They laid the groundwork for prog rock, influenced punk/ garage music, and inspired artists across many genres (including Bruce Springsteen, The Grateful Dead, and Rod Stewart, to name a few.)

The Kinks also formed in 1962, and their rise in popularity aligned with that of the British Invasion. They released their breakout hit “You Really Got Me” in 1964, quickly achieving international success. They were heavy hitters on the British Invasion circuit – that is, until 1966, when they were banned from touring in America. While the reason has never been explicitly made public, it is believed to be a combination of promoter disputes, chaotic behavior from the band, and visa and permit issues. Nevertheless, they continued to grow in popularity in the UK and America alike.

The Kinks’ satirical single “Sunday Afternoon” was inspired by British music hall – a form of working-class variety theater rooted in Victorian-era taverns and pubs. The track was the biggest hit in the UK during the summer of 1966. The Kinks are often regarded as the original punk band, with much of their music featuring fuzzy guitar distortion and a driving, bluesy energy. They influenced countless bands across rock subgenres, including psychedelic bands like The Doors and Jefferson Airplane, punk rock groups such as the Ramones, and Oasis and other Britpop bands.

The Who popularized many techniques and terms that would become touchstones in hard rock. Their incredible mastery of their instruments allowed them to get super creative with their recording and production. They experimented heavily with synthesizers and feedback, adding layers of texture that were unprecedented at the time. Guitarist and songwriter (and legend!) Pete Townshend had a massive impact on rock music unto himself. He popularized the use of power chords and also coined the term “power pop” in reference to the group’s sound. Townshend wrote the band’s most popular album, Tommy, which was the first time the term “rock opera” was used publicly.

Drummer Keith Moon also had a big hand in the band’s popularity and original sound. His playing was chaotic and unruly – he pushed the rhythm section out of the pocket and onto center stage. Moon was the backbone of The Who’s explosive, energetic live performances. They were the loudest band of the era, and maybe of all time – a quality that was understandably polarizing.

And now we’ve reached the greatest rock band of all time. The Rolling Stones (who are remarkably still together and actively performing) are pioneers of hard rock music. They began their career with blues covers (a common theme on this list) and were greatly influenced by American R&B artists such as Chuck Berry, Muddy Waters, and Fats Domino. The band was drawn to the sheer power and emotional expression these musicians brought to their work.

Eventually, the Stones moved on to writing and recording their own original music – tracks that would, conversely, influence the next generation of American blues and rock artists, a prime example of the circuitous and reciprocal nature of music. These songs were primarily written by Mick Jagger and Keith Richards, a collaboration that sometimes led to tension in the group. Not that it affected them much – they have countless international hits. The Stones’ expansive discography bridged the gap between rhythm and blues and hard classic rock, and also between America and the UK. Their swagger and flamboyant rebellion made them popular in youth culture on both sides of the Atlantic.

Bet you didn’t see this one coming – Beatlemania, baby! Formed in 1960, The Beatles are the most influential band in popular music. They started out with a clean-cut, boy-next-door look and sound – all pop, no grit. With time, they evolved into something much more. They began to travel and broaden their horizons, getting to know themselves and the world on a deeper level. Their lyrics grew deeper and more poetic, and their influences expanded vastly: Indian and Western classical music, pop, psychedelia, and folk were all incorporated. Their evolution as a band is something to be studied – and it has been, quite thoroughly and quite often.

The British Invasion would never have happened without The Beatles – they were the foot in the door to the American popular music market. They were the voice of youth culture and the counterculture, a living and breathing documentation of the Western world from 1960 to 1970. Their constant reinvention and unprecedented levels of success have never been matched or recreated, and likely never will.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

John Travolta Didn’t Recognize Himself as Alleged JFK Assassin Johnny Roselli in ‘November 1963’: ‘I Went, “Oh My God, He’s the Devil”’

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

John Travolta rarely connects with all his characters. Sometimes, he even loves to hate them.

“They say you have to find something you like,” the actor explains. “But I’ve never felt that. I think you have to like the result, pulling it off. That’s where the joy comes from. To become these people, you don’t have to identify with them or enjoy being them. You just have to be excellent at executing them.” Related Stories ‘SNL’ Sets Timothée Chalamet and Robert De Niro as Final Hosts of 2026 ‘SNL’: Druski Dons Whiteface as Elizabeth Holmes in ‘You Can See Everything’ Parody

Even so, Travolta faced one of the biggest challenges of his career playing a rather literal executioner in Roland Joffé’s “November 1963,” which opened the Torino Film Festival with its international premiere.

On paper, Johnny Roselli seems right in Travolta’s wheelhouse: a real-life wiseguy turned showbiz fixer, an Al Capone protégé whose career took him from Hollywood to Las Vegas to the Bay of Pigs, leaving a trail of bodies along the way.

“He was an odd mix,” says Travolta. “He was the guy who asked Sinatra to connect Kennedy with the mob. He hooked Kennedy up with Marilyn Monroe. He was a Hollywood icon of sorts, and had this other, darker side as a hitman. That’s complex. I’ve done dark subject matter before, but this was real. It actually happened, and that’s the difference.”

Roselli was purportedly a CIA go-between and sharpshooter. “November 1963” takes that history a step further, imagining him as a key triggerman on the Grassy Knoll, directly responsible for the killing that shook a nine-year-old boy in Englewood, New Jersey, and still haunts the actor to this day.

“It was the biggest crime of the century,” says Travolta, instantly transported back to second grade. “The announcement came at 11 a.m. Eastern Standard Time that the President had been shot, and then three hours later, at 2 p.m., they confirmed that he had passed. We all went to our homes, and all I can remember is being home with my father, glued to the television, sitting on his lap and trying to make sense of it all.”

Travolta carried that weight onto the set, where, he says, he was asked “to play a very evil man with moments of humanity.”

“The actors were all suffering because everybody who was hired was a very decent person, and we all had to play, to some degree, people who were not,” he continues. “So it was harder to go home every night. It took a glass of wine and a nice dinner to snap out of the zone.”

That feeling returned in full force when Travolta finally watched his finished performance at Torino’s opening night, prompting a kind of out-of-body experience.

“I thought, ‘Wow, I can’t believe that’s me,’” he says. “I’ve never seen myself lose my soul in a film, and [here] I was soulless. I was very proud that I could portray that, because it’s against my very essence. When I looked at it on screen, I went, ‘Oh my god, he’s the devil.’”

Written and produced by family members of Windy City crime boss Sam Giancana, “November 1963” foregrounds the Chicago Outfit’s role in the assassination plot, leaving CIA and government involvement for a possible follow-up.

“This is not history; it’s about human beings,” Travolta says. “There will be lots more to tell, but you can’t do it all at the same time.”

That deliberately incomplete picture appealed to Joffé, who translates the murky history into a mise-en-scène of skewed angles and funhouse reflections.

“That’s how history comes to us,” he says. “I’d say this story is probably 90% true, insofar as no one will ever know what really happened. What’s most important to me is whether it’s credible. And has that mindset changed? The transactional mindset, the mafia mindset, the idea that power is all that counts and loyalty has to be bought at the point of a gun. My feeling is that it doesn’t disappear. It transmutes. It may become a political party. It may become the way business acts.”

In one telling revision, the film casts slain Dallas policeman J.D. Tippit, whose murder was attributed to Lee Harvey Oswald, as a key conspirator. Joffé uses the twist to further muddy the waters while dismissing the notion of a neatly orchestrated plot.

“I reject the conspiracy theory,” he says. “If it were a conspiracy, we would have known by now, and we absolutely do not. What it was is collusion, and collusion is very different. Collusion is subtle. It happens in the shadows. It’s a nod, a wink, a knowing glance. It’s putting a gun in the right place.”

The distinction, he argues, is that no grand design is required. The CIA and organized crime had already worked together on plots to kill Fidel Castro, but that didn’t mean anyone had to convene a committee to plan Kennedy’s assassination.

“It’s not about control,” Joffé says. “It’s about the fact that life is chaotic, and the killing of the President comes out of that. We made a film about chaos.”

Your Flight Takes 10 Minutes Longer Than It Used To—And That’s A Luxury Worth Paying For

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Flying takes longer than it used to – and, retirement of the Concorde notwithstanding, it’s not because planes aren’t as fast. There are more than twice as many commercial flights as there were at the time of airline deregulation, and air traffic control hasn’t been upgraded very much. There’s congested airspace and also congested airports. We also don’t build major new airports – the last one was Denver, 30 years ago. (Austin’s new 1999 airport was a converted military base, and there were others like that in the 90s.) By one estimate flights have gotten 8% longer. That means a two hour flight is 10 minutes longer than it used to be. And the single biggest factor is often tagged as strategic padding of schedules. Airlines are often chided for ‘schedule padding’ – claiming that flights take longer than they should. Often, this just reflects the reality of telling customers when they can expect to arrive – although there’s greater variance than there used to be, which isn’t great for having a gate available when you land early. Airlines do not like this! Though real airfares (even including fees) are down significantly since deregulation, longer flight times are expensive for staffing, fuel and efficient use of $50 – $100 million planes. And longer travel times historically disadvantaged an airline trying to sell tickets, because it meant their flights were displayed lower on the screen that travel agents and later passengers directly saw – which translates into fewer sales. A new study finds that airlines with more “padding” in their schedules tend to charge higher fares. That sounds like passengers paying more for a slower trip. But those extra minutes can also let United hold your plane with ConnectionSaver and get you home that night, and still arrive on time. It also means fewer delays and missed connections. So that ‘premium’ is actually a more valuable product to many consumers. I’ve argued for years that schedule padding often isn’t really padding. Sometimes an airline is finally admitting how long the trip takes. Customers can plan around an honest arrival time. There’s a new chapter by Embry-Riddle’s Jules Yimga, “The Price of Punctuality,” in The Economics of Airline Pricing. It combines 2019 flight time data with the government’s ticket price survey. It compares schedules against different percentiles of actual flight times, then examines how the resulting measures relate to fares. More padding is associated with higher fares, especially when the comparison uses the faster end of actual flight times. The relationship appears in both competitive and concentrated markets. The abstract itself allows that reliability could be a service customers value. (Interestingly, Yimga’s earlier research on schedule padding attributed higher prices primarily to higher costs rather than higher margins.) Even scheduling to the median arrival time means roughly half of trips take longer than published. It’s often better for a customer if an airline underpromises and overdelivers – or arrives ‘on time’ rather than late. Predictability matters when you’re scheduling connections or trying to arrive in time for a meeting. An airline that leaves little buffer is going to appear less reliable, so customers book earlier flights and longer layovers or even fly in the night before an important meeting. Then the buffer hasn’t disappeared, it just changes who’s scheduling it. That’s why I argued American’s investment in more realistic schedules could pay for itself. Avoiding rebooking, mishandled bags and aircraft and crews getting out of position can justify spending running an on-time schedule even before a customer chooses the airline again because the trip met expectations. American’s Dallas – Fort Worth overhaul that began in April combined longer scheduled flight times with a move from nine daily banks of flights to thirteen. Spreading flights into smaller waves reduced congestion that made some of the extra time unnecessary. One plane waiting for a gate can delay its passengers, its next departure, and other flights whose crews are stuck on board. Giving the operation room to recover can prevent that problem from cascading. In January, I covered American network planning chief Brian Znotins explaining that bookings for 40–54 minute connections at DFW were 4% below what its model predicted. Bookings for 55–84 minute connections were 8% above prediction. Customers have actually started choosing longer connections because they feel more reliable and less likely to leave them stranded. When I covered United’s media day in 2019, Greg Hart explained that 25% of its flights each day were expected to arrive ten minutes early. That gave United room to use ConnectionSaver to hold flights for connecting passengers and still arrive on time. Of course there’s such a thing as too much slack. An airline can improve its reported punctuality by moving the promised arrival later without getting anyone anywhere sooner. Excessively long connections waste customer time. And routinely beating an absurdly generous schedule doesn’t make an operation excellent. Not all padding is something customers will pay a premium for. I’ve argued for years that schedule padding often isn’t really padding. Sometimes an airline is finally admitting how long the trip takes. Customers can plan around an honest arrival time.

There’s a new chapter by Embry-Riddle’s Jules Yimga, “The Price of Punctuality,” in The Economics of Airline Pricing. It combines 2019 flight time data with the government’s ticket price survey. It compares schedules against different percentiles of actual flight times, then examines how the resulting measures relate to fares.

More padding is associated with higher fares, especially when the comparison uses the faster end of actual flight times. The relationship appears in both competitive and concentrated markets. The abstract itself allows that reliability could be a service customers value. (Interestingly, Yimga’s earlier research on schedule padding attributed higher prices primarily to higher costs rather than higher margins.)

Even scheduling to the median arrival time means roughly half of trips take longer than published. It’s often better for a customer if an airline underpromises and overdelivers – or arrives ‘on time’ rather than late. Predictability matters when you’re scheduling connections or trying to arrive in time for a meeting.

An airline that leaves little buffer is going to appear less reliable, so customers book earlier flights and longer layovers or even fly in the night before an important meeting. Then the buffer hasn’t disappeared, it just changes who’s scheduling it.

That’s why I argued American’s investment in more realistic schedules could pay for itself. Avoiding rebooking, mishandled bags and aircraft and crews getting out of position can justify spending running an on-time schedule even before a customer chooses the airline again because the trip met expectations.

American’s Dallas – Fort Worth overhaul that began in April combined longer scheduled flight times with a move from nine daily banks of flights to thirteen. Spreading flights into smaller waves reduced congestion that made some of the extra time unnecessary.

One plane waiting for a gate can delay its passengers, its next departure, and other flights whose crews are stuck on board. Giving the operation room to recover can prevent that problem from cascading.

In January, I covered American network planning chief Brian Znotins explaining that bookings for 40–54 minute connections at DFW were 4% below what its model predicted. Bookings for 55–84 minute connections were 8% above prediction. Customers have actually started choosing longer connections because they feel more reliable and less likely to leave them stranded.

When I covered United’s media day in 2019, Greg Hart explained that 25% of its flights each day were expected to arrive ten minutes early. That gave United room to use ConnectionSaver to hold flights for connecting passengers and still arrive on time.

Of course there’s such a thing as too much slack. An airline can improve its reported punctuality by moving the promised arrival later without getting anyone anywhere sooner. Excessively long connections waste customer time. And routinely beating an absurdly generous schedule doesn’t make an operation excellent. Not all padding is something customers will pay a premium for.

Wizards Is Only Targeting Counterfeit Cards, Not Regular MTG Proxies

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

There's a fine line between a proxy and a counterfeit card, and Wizards is only interested in eradicating one of them from the game of Magic.

A wave of controversy hit Magic: The Gathering this week after Wizards of the Coast revealed it is taking legal action against a group of proxy card businesses, collectively referred to as 'PrintMTG Enterprise,' that produce and sell unofficial MTG cards directly to players on several marketplaces. The lawsuit is worth $28.5 million, and it includes strict conditions if Wizards succeeds, such as handing over all card-printing equipment used in the business.

The community was immediately up in arms over the announcement, as it felt like Wizards was taking a huge step forward in a war against proxy cards, but the reality is that your average proxy card is safe, and this is actually more about counterfeit cards. Is there a difference? Yes, and it could be a costly one for 'PrintMTG Enterprises.'

The difference between a counterfeit Magic card and a proxy is that a proxy card is clearly not a real Magic card, typically because it has a plain back or doesn't use official Magic artwork on the front. In most cases, proxy cards have a plain white back, so they can't be confused with an official Magic card.

Counterfeit cards are carbon copies of the original Magic design and are intended to deceive players who aren't all that familiar with the minor details that make a Magic card legit. This can also include using the same paper stock as official Magic cards to make them feel authentic in hand, as well as any details meant to fool the eye.

It's entirely fair for Wizards of the Coast to target any seller looking to reproduce Magic cards and sell them in a condition that could be used to defraud others, and this lawsuit shouldn't be confused with Wizards taking a stance against proxy makers or users.

If you're looking for the best Secret Lair MTG Commander deck to purchase on the secondary market, we're ranking them all from worst to best.

One of the big debates that arose from this lawsuit is why now? It's fair to say that counterfeit Magic cards have been around for a long time, so why is now the time for Wizards to take a stance and look to get them off the market?

The reality is that we'll never know why, but it's a positive move for the secondary market regardless. Proxy cards, when advertised and produced correctly, aren't mistaken for regular Magic cards, and those who buy them are 100% okay with that.

Counterfeit cards become a problem when people look on typically trusted marketplaces for an official card but end up buying a counterfeit version without knowing it, because the seller is trying to make a quick buck.

This isn't to say that the companies producing these counterfeit cards do so with the intent of deceiving players in online transactions, but that's what is happening, and it needs to stop.

I've only ever purchased one counterfeit card in my time playing Magic, and it was a copy of Tibalt's Trickery. It wasn't a very expensive card, but the copy I received was clearly fake, not to the eye, but to the touch. I reported the seller, who had ghosted me after the transaction, and I got my money back, but I wonder how many players that seller managed to fool.

On the proxy front, I only own one set of proxy cards: the Goblin Storm Secret Lair Commander deck. Due to this deck selling out incredibly fast and being scalped for insane prices online, I decided to proxy it instead, but did so from a seller who clearly stated that the backs of the cards would arrive plain white, and that the paper stock used was quite thin (as a warning for when I sleeved the deck). This was perfect, because I planned to use the deck still at my local store, and with my Commander pod, I wanted to be able to say "hey, this is a proxy, does anyone have a problem with that?" as the code of honor among most groups is that, as long as you admit to what it is, there's typically no issue playing with it.

Magic is a very expensive game to get into in most formats, so proxies let you avoid extortionate secondary market prices (especially on land for Commander right now) and still play with limited-availability stuff like Goblin Storm.

Proxies are great, and they should continue to be allowed as long as certain rules are followed. I have no issue with Wizards setting a clear boundary for what a counterfeit card is and why it isn't good for the game.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

The 5 Biggest Airbus A220 Fleets In 2026 Don't Even Include The Jet's Launch Operator

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Air France took delivery of its 60th Airbus A220-300 on November 6, closing out a five-year narrowbody renewal program. That milestone underscores the regional jet's growing global fleet and its place among the world's largest operators. Joining the French flag carrier on that podium are two airlines that exclusively fly the A220: Breeze Airways, with 57 and growing, and airBaltic's 55-strong fleet.

The budget carriers on opposite sides of the Atlantic Ocean join Delta Air Lines, which boasts 92 as of now, and JetBlue Airways with 65, to round out the top five. Notably, SWISS International Air Lines, which was the launch customer for the airframe, still owns just 30 aircraft. The jet's difficult development and launch after Airbus acquired the platform from Bombardier is just one part of the story.

The A220 began as the CSeries before Airbus acquired a controlling stake in 2018. The Swiss order ended after it replaced its aging Avro RJ100 regional fleet, partly due to the ongoing engine crisis that has plagued the plane despite the otherwise popular airframe. Persistent durability issues and supply-chain bottlenecks with the Pratt & Whitney PW1500G engines forced operators worldwide to ground jets and even strip some for viable powerplants, keeping as many flying as possible.

Latvian flag carrier airBaltic introduced the stretched A220-300 in December 2016 and retired every turboprop and Boeing 737 by 2020 to run a pure single-type fleet. The most recent data places the carrier fifth globally with 55 aircraft listed on its roster, according to Airport Spotting. However, not all of those are airworthy. Groundings peaked at 13 parked jets in early 2025, wiping out thousands of seasonal flights across Europe and forcing costly replacement wet leases.

Restructuring plans shrink the active fleet from 54 jets to 36 by year-end and put 40 undelivered orders under review, even though the airframe itself never caused the crisis. Operated purely on its merits, the airframe exceeded expectations across both scheduled flying and wet lease contracts. Its low fuel burn let airBaltic restart routes profitably at lower passenger loads after the pandemic while leasing up to 13 aircraft to SWISS and Lufthansa Group partners.

Flying one 145-seat cabin simplified crew training, cut maintenance complexity, and supported flights stretching 3,450 nautical miles (6,389 km) from Riga to leisure markets like Tenerife. The vulnerability sat beneath the wing, where the A220 certifies only Pratt & Whitney's PW1500G geared turbofan.

When metal defects in turbine blades triggered urgent inspections and stretched engine overhauls past 200 days, airBaltic had no alternative supplier to fall back on. Engine availability recovered early this year, yet years of grounded aircraft left airBaltic financially fragile when unhedged fuel bills spiked following the recent conflict in Iran. On September 14, the carrier filed for Chapter 11 bankruptcy protection in New York, naming Pratt as its largest creditor.

SWISS will retire its pioneering A220-100 fleet as engine shortages favor the larger A220-300.

Air France secured third place globally on November 6 when its 60th A220-300 arrived in Paris before opening service to Prague three days later, per Airline Geeks. That handover fulfilled the 2019 commitment to retire outdated Airbus A318 and A319 aircraft serving its network. Five years after entering service, the 148-seat fleet has carried nearly 24 million passengers and established the A220-300 as Air France's new medium-haul mainstay.

Finishing the original batch roughly a year behind its initial 2025 target still left four 20-year-old A318s on the winter schedule. Industry-wide delivery delays and Pratt's PW1500G recalls tightened spare availability. As a result, Air France extended A318 operations to March 27, 2027. Rather than stopping at 60 jets, however, Paris now anticipates a 61st A220's arrival and is keeping 30 options plus 30 purchase rights.

Route economics and passenger demand are driving Air France-KLM Group leadership to envision growing toward 90 or 95 A220s. Its five-abreast cabin puts 80% of travelers in a window or aisle seat with 20% more overhead bin volume, while free high-speed WiFi already reaches 85% of the fleet, with full installation expected by February 2027.

The modern jet could pose stark competitive pressure in the long-term.

JetBlue holds second place globally after counting 66 A220-300s in October out of its 100-aircraft total order, according to the Center for Aviation. That expansion completed the carrier's September 2025 phase-out of the Embraer E190. Trading 100-seat E190s for 140-seat A220-300s cut operating costs per seat by 29% and fuel burn per seat by 40%, projecting $4 million to $5 million in annual gains per jet.

Meanwhile, passengers benefit from bigger seats measuring 18.6 inches (47 centimeters) wide, the roomiest economy seating in JetBlue's fleet. That comes alongside 35 inches (89 centimeters) of pitch in extra-legroom rows and 32 inches (81 centimeters) in standard rows. The jets also have upgraded seatback screens and free high-speed WiFi.

Even at scale, JetBlue saw disruption from Pratt's engine recall as well as the Airbus factory bottlenecks in Mirabel and Mobile. Fiscal reports showed the airline opened the year with four aircraft parked over PW1500G shortages. After engine removal, the planes spent roughly 200 days in overhaul shops. Supply-chain delays also forced Airbus to trim JetBlue's delivery schedule, pushing its final three firm aircraft into 2030 and beyond.

Eliminating the E190 still consolidated JetBlue into an exclusively Airbus operator, streamlining pilot and mechanic qualifications alongside its A320 and A321neo fleets. The transition also unlocked longer transcontinental routes for its smallest aircraft.

These airlines are the world's three largest operators of the type.

Breeze climbed to fourth worldwide when its final Embraer E190 revenue flight landed in New Orleans from Orlando on September 7, following acceptance of the 57th A220 in July, per Airport Spotting. This autumn deliveries pushed the Salt Lake City carrier over 60 jets out of 90 firm orders and 30 options for more. That fleet blueprint follows the design of Chief Executive David Neeleman when he ordered 60 jets in 2018, right after Airbus took over the program.

Neeleman revisited the decision in an Aerospace Global News story in September of this year, saying: "Our long-term strategy has always been to leverage the strengths and efficiencies of the Airbus A220-300 to help us cost-effectively scale our unique business model which centers around offering affordable, nonstop flights from unserved and underserved markets."

Breeze fits 137 seats inside the A220-300, pairing 125 economy chairs behind 12 Ascent first class recliners pitched at 39 inches (99 centimeters), flying 91 cities across more than 300 routes where 85% to 90% carry zero nonstop rivals. That 3,400 nautical miles (6,297 km) reach outclasses the Embraer E195-E2 and older Boeing 737-700s, opening cross-border flights to Cancun, Punta Cana, Montego Bay, Nassau, Costa Rica, and Santo Domingo.

Running one modern airframe slashes spare parts inventory and simplifies pilot scheduling compared with airlines splitting narrowbody flying across multiple cockpits. Meanwhile, the premium front cabin fills faster on thin leisure routes than any other regional jet could match. The airline has seen its 'long and thin' city pairs become steadily more profitable routes as a result.

A high-density model is set to enter service next year.

Delta Air Lines has consistently led the global rankings by a wide margin, with 87 counted in July before third-quarter figures raised its roster to 92 jets, per Aviation Daily Report. Unlike the single-variant fleets run by JetBlue, Breeze, Air France, and airBaltic, Delta flies a split roster of 45 A220-100s averaging seven years old and 47 newer A220-300s with 145 firm orders.

Atlanta built its fleet on an order of 75 Bombardier CS100s from April 2016. That triggered Boeing's 2017 trade complaint, which was later linked to the proposed 292% US tariff. Chief Executive Ed Bastian held firm in October 2017, according to Reuters, saying, "We are not going to pay any tariff, and we do expect to take the airplanes," just before Bombardier transferred majority control to Airbus and set up final assembly in Mobile, Alabama.

US trade commissioners unanimously threw out those tariffs in January 2018, clearing Delta to launch A220 service from New York's LaGuardia. Today, it deploys two variants from coast to coast as 25-year-old Boeing 717-200s and even older A319s are phased out. Looking ahead, the gap between top operators will widen further as Airbus eyes a 2027 launch decision on a stretched Airbus A220-500. The new variant would seat 160 to 185 passengers, with deliveries targeting 13 jets per month from 2028.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Opinion: Liverpool will sign perfect player for Andoni Iraola, he'll only cost £19m

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Andoni Iraola has done a pretty decent job since being appointed as Liverpool's manager.

He's managed to get his team to fourth in the Premier League table with only seven points separating them from first-placed Arsenal.

Obviously, this is still a decent number of points to chip away at but, if the Gunners continue dropping points by drawing games against teams such as Newcastle, the Reds could find themselves back in the title race soon.

Nobody knows what lies ahead for any team but, if Liverpool manage to pull off even half of the transfers they've been linked with, they'll be storming their way up the table.

And to make things even sweeter, the perfect signing for Iraola has just emerged.

Arhcie Brown has a £19.2 million release clause in his contract

So many areas among Liverpool's ranks still need upgrading.

This is a horrible thing to write as, across the last couple of years, well over £600 million has been spent on new players by the Reds.

Some of the transfers that they've made have worked out for the better but, others haven't quite hit the mark.

Milos Kerkez is an example of this because, even though he has had a fair few decent performances, his inconsistency is terrible.

Some days he can be seen as the Premier League's best left-back and, on others, he could end up being the worst player on the pitch.

This has forced fans to ask for a new left-back with Florian Plettenberg now sharing a rather intriguing update on Liverpool target, Archie Brown.

"Archie Brown has a release clause worth around €23m, valid for both the winter and summer transfer windows. The 23 y/o is a regular starter at Fenerbahçe and has also impressed with some very strong performances in the Champions League," he wrote.

"Under contract until 2028, a move is planned, with Premier League clubs already making enquiries."

Archie Brown would fit straight into Andoni Iraola's plans

Not only would Brown be classed as homegrown but, he'd also be one of the cheapest transfers on the market.

Recent links with Liverpool and Alejandro Balde have emerged with Barcelona demanding close to €50 million for the Spaniard.

Others such as Daniel Svensson have also been linked with a move but, again, Borussia Dortmund want over €40 million for him.

Each of these players would be exceptional additions but, Brown for €23 million (£19 million) is a deal that's too good to pass up.

He put in the performance of a lifetime when Liverpool faced Fenerbahce earlier in the season and, if he can replicate that form in the Premier League, the Reds would be onto a winner.

Liverpool signing Brown would also give Kerkez a genuine challenger for his position which might even help him develop his performance.

Since the price for Brown is so good, Liverpool will have to act fast and get straight on with securing his services when the January transfer window opens up, otherwise, they'll end up kicking themselves for missing out on it.

Investing - Theory, News &amp; General • Re: When the market tanks, what do you plan to do in retirement?

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A lot of people here have much more money than the average person in retirement. Many only have social security and/or a small pension so money in the market for some is nil. For others all they have is some money invested and a big drop would be a disaster.

In our case once I got near retirement and now in retirement, I found that for me not to constantly think about market drops was to set up enough money in treasuries/TIPs and MYGAs so that the money in the stock market is of limited need for us. It would certainly hurt looking at a big drop and knowing it might not recover in our lifetimes but at this point my estimate is the withdrawals from the market account would only be around 2% in our 70s so a 50% drop would only bump that to 4%.

I can recall reading a ton of messages in the past where people would point out how stocks always out gain bonds and other fixed income so they would just keep it in the market but w/o other sources of income or just having a gazillion dollars there is no way I would be comfortable doing that.

Statistics: Posted by rich126 — Thu Nov 26, 2026 7:33 am

→ Baca artikel asli

Personal Investments • Re: investing in Gold

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It took gold until 2010 (30 years) to return to its inflation-adjusted 1980 high:

https://testfol.io/?s=60BUg2RV0dC

That's most of an investing lifetime. Not worth it IMO.
Everyone likes to point to 1980, but that was an outlier. Yes gold is volatile, but so are stocks. Are stocks a bad investment because of how they did if you purchased them 1929?

How did gold fare if you bought it in 1970? Or 1975?

Personally I bought some gold when the government was handing out Covid checks. At that point it was obvious to me we would see inflation. It worked out for me.

Is it an investment like stocks? Absolutely not.
Because the price of gold was fixed in USD terms 1945-71, (Bretton Woods to President Nixon) it's questionable to use those data points.

What the track record says is that gold is highly volatile & tricky as an inflation hedge for that reason. It may (or may not) serve as a long term hedge, and as a countercyclical asset.

I suggest Campbell Harvey at Duke University business school, for some good articles on gold.

Statistics: Posted by Valuethinker — Thu Nov 26, 2026 7:33 am

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Is withdrawal rate modeling too simplistic to be useful to most people?

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It is impossible to plan spending beyond a couple of years in my opinion and it just becomes mental gymnastics. 20 to 25x expenses saved is a good goal for many yet most people never achieve that level of financial wealth.

If your annual withdrawals are somewhere between 4 to 6% of your liquid assets you have nothing to worry about. If the withdrawals go above 6%, cut spending (I compare this to being laid off) or if it goes below 4% you have the option to spend more (think of it as getting a big bonus from work).

We deal with bumpy expenses all throughout our life so I am not sure why it needs to be difference once one retires. The only thing that changes is that you are no longer exchanging your time for dollars and your investments are providing the income you need. Your cash buffer is there as an emergency fund. Again I wouldn't think of this any different than when I was working. If I need a roof next year, I am not buying a new car or going on an expensive vacation. If you have saved 20-25x your annual expenses, it means you were a good saver and you already know how to manage your personal finances.

I think of my investments as a business. I hold equity in companies that make products/provide services and share part of their profit with me. I lend money to companies and the government through the bond funds I hold and get paid interest. I built this business through my labor throughout the years and now an absentee owner. Once a year figure out how the business did last year and determine a withdrawal amount depending on how it performed. Again no different than how most of us operate during our working careers or how small business owners run their finances.

Vet Analyst Sets Ethereum Floor Ahead Of $1,755 Recovery

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Home » Ethereum (ETH) » Vet Analyst Sets Ethereum Floor Ahead Of $1,755 Recovery

Futures data hints at bulls regaining control over Ether’s price, but seasoned analysts hint at a bigger correction.

Chartist Peter Brandt highlighted a multi-year triangle form on Ethereum’s (ETH) weekly chart, with the cryptocurrency approaching the lower boundary of the pattern. The $1,383 level is crucial due to the long-term support floor holding the threshold, usually backed by both retail & crypto whales.

Peter Brandt’s cautious look on Ethereum’s (ETH) near term price matches with Peter Schiff’s recent ETH price prediction towards a $1,000 slump. He even predicted a further slump that could lead to a 70% deficit if the market doesn’t adjust to geopolitical issues & stock chaos.

If $ETH cannot remain above 1383 I see no reason why price cannot go back to sub-$500 pic.twitter.com/OT9hQ3lBFU

With Bitcoin (BTC) backtracking to mid $57,000 levels, the rest of the market followed by plunging way below the crucial moving averages. Right now, Ethereum (ETH) is situated between the lower & mid envelopes of the Bollinger Band, putting the $1,699 level in focus.

Other analysts treat the $1,550 price region as a potential base rather than the beginning of a much deeper breakdown, while Futures data perfectly aligns with that theory. Short-sellers are dominating liquidations again, while the long versus short ratio hits above 1 on most exchanges.

Given that the largest altcoin maintains that level on the 4-hour charts, the $1,755.58 Red-Label Bollinger Band comes into play. That level will be hard to restore while the Chaikin Money Flow (CMF) still dwells in red figures. A push above 0 could restore long-term holder faith in ETH.

Also, the Ethereum-based exchange-traded funds (ETFs) have been dropping millions of dollars a day, dropping $217.50 million since last week. The weekly ETH ETF flows are now on a 9-week losing streak. Last time the ETF flows turned positive on September 25, 2026.

Wall Street’s sell-off sentiment didn’t scare away some famous crypto whales: BitMine’s Tom Lee acquired a massive 7,819 ETH, taking the company’s total holdings to 6.09 million ETH, summing up to 4.98% of Ethereum’s total supply as the altcoin hovers around $1,699.

Fifa hit by cyberattack amid mounting scrutiny of Gianni Infantino

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

The governing body has not disclosed when the breach occurred or the full extent of the compromised data

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

World football’s governing body, Fifa, has been targeted in a cyberattack that compromised what it describes as a "limited set of personal and business information," it confirmed on Thursday.

"Fifa is working closely with the relevant authorities, including law enforcement," the organisation stated in a message.

Fifa did not reveal when the cyberattack occurred or provide the precise nature of the breached data.

Last week, a group calling itself the Handsome Boy Hacking Club posted a screenshot on X of what it claimed were Fifa files. Three days later, the group appeared to take credit when responding to a post stating that more than 12 gigabytes of internal documents had been leaked.

"FIFA condemns in the strongest possible terms the unlawful acquisition and publication of information, particularly personal data," the governing body said in its statement. "Such actions constitute a serious and unacceptable violation of privacy and can have significant consequences for the individuals concerned."

The breach occurs at a time when Fifa and its president, Gianni Infantino, face heightened scrutiny following a foiled, secretive plan to sell stakes in the World Cup to private investors.

Infantino is also attempting to extend his 11-year presidency of Fifa to 2031. He remains the favourite when the election is held on March 18, with Kohzo Tashima of Japan and Philippe Diallo of France standing as the other candidates.

TSA Demanded United Airlines Suspend A Pilot Over Terror Support, Union Accused Of Resisting, Report Says

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

TSA reportedly told United Airlines to suspend a pilot accused of supporting a terrorist group, after the pilots union resisted action according to a federal government source cited by TV news reporter Mike Wagenheim. The new report says the intervention happened last week. The pilot hasn’t been named. Neither has the terror group he allegedly supported. Mike Wagenheim’s November 24 update on the reported TSA intervention Wagenheim’s earlier report cited two sources saying United knew of up to six additional crew members who’d expressed support for terrorist groups. United’s pilots union spokesman declined to discuss individual cases. He said the Air Line Pilots Association has a professional standards committee and legal team that assess accusations against its ethics code and United’s workplace guidelines. The Pilot Israel Barred Was Already On Leave In October, Israel prohibited flights with Sina Amjadian serving as crew from arriving in the country. Israeli officials confirmed the restriction after receiving allegations that he’d expressed support for Iran’s Revolutionary Guards and Hezbollah. But United said he wasn’t currently flying, having been on leave since 2024. He flew Boeing 737s and wasn’t qualified on long haul aircraft. Apparently there was material showing Iranian leadership imagery and mourning civilians killed in strikes. There are claims of Hezbollah support and that there was a video of him kissing the regime’s emblem. United Pilot Celebrated October 7 Rapes And Murders As ‘Bravery’ I first wrote about Ibrahim Mossallam in November 2023, after his Facebook description of the October 7 attack as “resistance by a brave people.” United removed him from service with pay but he was reported back flying. On October 6, 2026, after the flydubai pilot sought to use his plane as a missile to attack Israel, United told me: We have withheld this pilot from service pending an ongoing review. There were also reports that he returned after just a day, and that two colleagues had given up first class seats to monitor him in the cockpit. United subsequently denied both accounts. A reported claimed the union blocked his firing. TSA Can Order A Pilot Out Of The Cockpit It was striking that the reported order came from TSA. It has authority that extends well beyond passenger checkpoints. Under federal law, a TSA determination that a pilot poses, or is suspected of posing, a terrorism or passenger safety threat requires the Federal Aviation Administration to take action against the pilot’s certificate. TSA can request that the action take effect immediately. It can also issue mandatory security directives to airlines. However, it isn’t clear from the report what legal mechanism is claimed to have been used and doesn’t report an FAA certificate action. It says the pilot’s “TSA credential” was revoked and describes that as Known Crewmember access. TSA can require an airline to remove someone’s access, but losing that privilege doesn’t mean losing a pilot’s license. Flydubai Shows Why The Concern Is Real I don’t need to know a pilot’s politics. But I wouldn’t want someone who celebrates the killing of civilians at the controls of my flight. That calls their judgment into question even without evidence they’ve planned an attack themselves. The September 30 flydubai cockpit attack makes the concern concrete. The co-pilot of that flight planned a suicide attack on the Tel Aviv airport, attacking the captain with an axe to take over the aircraft. He had satisfied the licensing, good conduct and security requirements for the job, and those checks had failed to uncover his extremist intentions. The values of the person in control of an aircraft matter. A Used Cybertruck Is Selling A Month Faster Than A Ford F-150

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

If you had to guess which used pickup found a buyer fastest in October, the Toyota Tacoma would be a sensible bet. It’s America’s best-selling midsize truck and has a reputation for holding its value. Or you might pick the super frugal Ford Maverick Hybrid. But the truck that beat them both was the Tesla Cybertruck.

The data from the new iSeeCars study covers 1.1 million used vehicles that changed hands in October, so this was before Tesla announced it would discontinue the Cybertruck in 2027. The study looked at one- to five-year-old vehicles and ranked models by how long they’d been listed before selling.

The Cybertruck surpassed all other pickups, averaging 39.1 days, compared to 65.5 days for used pickups overall. The Maverick Hybrid came closest at 43.3 days, followed by the Rivian R1T at 45.2 and the F-150 Lightning at 47.4. The Tacoma came in fifth position.

The gap between the two electric pickups at either end of the list was even more striking. A used Chevrolet Silverado EV took 107.5 days to sell, putting it last among the 24 trucks in the study. That’s a 68-day difference between two American electric pickups. Across the wider used market, EVs averaged about 46 days to find a buyer, around nine fewer than hybrids and 15 fewer than gas vehicles.

How quickly a used Cybertruck sold seems to have changed a lot since May 2024, when some sellers were cutting as much as $30,000 from their asking prices after listing their trucks for close to $200,000. By December of that year, we reported that used Cybertrucks were piling up on dealer lots, with data at the time showing they stayed for 75 days, up from 27 in May.

Then owners got a look at what Tesla itself would pay for a used Cybertruck trade-in. One year later, in May 2025, one owner of a $99,990 dual-motor Foundation Series truck got a trade-in estimate of $65,400 after driving it just 6,211 miles.

So even though the Cybertruck was the fastest-selling used pickup in iSeeCars’ new study, it didn’t report what buyers paid, so we don’t know whether owners got their asking price or had to lower it to sell.

Tesla will build the last Cybertrucks at the end of summer 2027, and this will reshape their used market. Elon Musk originally announced production would end around the middle of the year, but then extended the run to fill more orders. And if you order a Cybertruck now, the only one you can get is the $110,000 tri-motor Cyberbeast, so anyone who wants a less expensive dual-motor truck has to look for a used example.

Once the last new Cybertruck is sold, I’d expect collectors and everyday truck buyers to look at used ones differently. Someone after a clean Foundation Series truck with low miles might pay a little extra for one close to home, especially since it could be months before another comes up for sale nearby.

At the same time, someone who just needs an electric pickup to tow a trailer has more options. If a used dual-motor Cybertruck costs about the same as a Ford F-150 Lightning or a Rivian R1T, I suspect that buyer will care more about mileage, condition, and how much battery warranty is left than when Tesla built its last truck.

Lions vs Bears live updates, TV channel, start time, how to watch Thanksgiving game

📅 Nov 26, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Consider the Detroit Lions vs. Chicago Bears matchup the charcuterie board of the Thanksgiving schedule.

As is tradition, the Lions (8-2) take the field on Turkey Day, this time playing NFC North rival Bears (8-2) to kick off the tripleheader of the day's games.

The Lions' path to 8-2 has certainly come with plenty of bumps along the way. The defense, which was among the league's worst early in the season, has course corrected and become stifling, allowing just 14.4 points per game (five games) following their Week 6 bye.

However, Detroit is going to have to find a way to continue navigating the remainder of the 2026 season without superstar pass rusher Aidan Hutchinson, who was injured in Week 10 while celebrating a sack.

The Bears' path to 8-2 hasn't always looked pretty, but's been effective. Offensively, a balanced Bears attack has led to a top-10 scoring offense designed by Ben Johnson and driven by Caleb Williams, but its been Chicago's suffocating stop unit that has helped guide Chicago to a critical NFC North matchup that could pay dividends come the end of the season.

It's a Turkey Day showdown between two bitter NFC North rivals. Here's what to know about the first of three Thanksgiving matchups:

Let's start with Swift's preferred autumnal color palette. She gravitates toward the season's rich jewel tones. Her style, which is often preppy or classic year-round with a vintage twist, includes paperboy hats, knee-high socks, colored tights, and plaid skirts. She's also embraced several of this season's trends, wearing wine-red hues, leather bomber jackets, and collegiate styles. So, it's safe to say both her current styling and vintage looks are encouraging me to upgrade my cold-weather fashion game. And while I am a fashion writer, you don't need to be a walking style encyclopedia—or global pop star at that—to master Swift's cold-weather ensembles.

Unlike the singer's glamorous red-carpet looks (I'm still not over that glittering ice-blue Dior gown from the 6th Annual Academy Museum Gala), her autumnal fashion is refreshingly down-to-earth, including attainable brands like Reformation and Anthropologie. Her ability to dress up simple staples like a black turtleneck with a few seasonal statement pieces has inspired how I think about pairing pieces in my own closet. Because her outfits are so normal, they're perfect for everything from a holiday party to a cozy date night. Ahead, I've rounded up my favorite Taylor Swift fall outfits of all time. You might as well hit play on Red while you're at it.

I’m still not over this preppy schoolboy-inspired look from 2023, which centers around a polo shirtdress and knee-high maple-hued boots. If it’s too chilly out to show your legs, simply slip on a pair of thermal tights. Keep the rest of the look simple with dainty jewelry. It’s one of those outfits that look effortlessly pieced together, hitting that sweet spot between casual and elevated.

Cynthia Rowley Cynthia Rowley Rugby Mini Dress

Creating an outfit that’s both cozy and chic enough for a dressy dinner can be tough—but this look from Swift is an easy solution. She layered a plush, oversized teddy coat over a simple, fitted long-sleeve top. Sheer tights under a plaid mini skirt dress the look up, while striking suede boots add more soft, autumnal texture.

Aritzia Original Contour Astral Longsleeve

For an elevated date night, I'd reach for a lace-trim slip dress. Swift made it cold-weather-approved with an oversized leather bomber jacket, which is one of this season's outerwear trends. While the pop singer opted for sandals, you can simply swap them out for black stiletto booties.

Charles & Keith Ally Ruched Chain-Handle Bag

Bordeaux red has become one of my favorite colors to style this season, and thanks to a co-sign from Swift, now I have even more inspiration. Swift’s towering cranberry heels serve as the visual focal point of this look, with a colorblocked polo and mixed-media pleated skirt from Thom Browne turning the outfit into a preppy maximalist’s dream.

You can take a break from the rich jewel tones every once in a while. Swift’s cloud-white ribbed mini dress is understated and sophisticated, with patent-leather accessories adding a subtle edge. Tie a bow around your ponytail for a girlish twist.
