We Officially Ranked the 8 Best Netflix Shows of All Time

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

As one of the biggest entertainment platforms in the world, Netflix has countless original series across practically every genre imaginable. From romance, sci-fi, horror, crime, the streaming giant has built a catalogue that has something for just about everyone. Of course, not every Netflix original is worth the binge, but every once in a while, the platform delivers a show that reminds us just how incredible episodic storytelling can be.

Here, we take a look at the best of the best in Netflix' vast library of shows. These series demonstrate the incredible range of storytelling and showcase great talents in front of and behind the cameras. Some will keep you glued to the screen with unexpected twists, while others will leave you thinking about their characters and themes for days. Spoiler alert: guilty pleasures or "second screen" shows most likely do not make this cut.

Based on John Steinbeck's novel, East of Eden follows the Trask family across multiple generations, starting with brothers Adam (Christopher Abbott) and Charles (Mike Faist) whose fortunes differ when their dad favors one over the other. Their lives changed when the mysterious Cathy Ames (Florence Pugh) arrives at their farm, influencing the decisions they make until later on in the future.

East of Eden is relatively new compared to the other series on this list but it is obvious that this show is one of the best things Netflix has put out in years. Zoe Kazan adapted this classic with care and respect, carrying her grandfather's legacy who adapted the third act of the novel with James Dean over 70 years ago. The thematic exploration of good and evil as well as the generational family dynamics are so satisfying and thorough. The show is further elevated by strong performances from the three leads, particularly Pugh. It also has scene-stealing performances from Hoon Lee, Ciaran Hinds and the new Hunger Games star Joseph Zada. It is undoubtedly one of the best literary adaptations ever made.

Set in the late 1970s, Mindhunter follows FBI agents Holden Ford (Jonathan Groff) and Bill Tench (Holt McCallany) as they research criminal profiling and psychology by interviewing serial killers. Working alongside psychologist Wendy Carr (Anna Torv), the two agents attempt to establish a new investigative discipline by examining the motivations and behaviors of some of the deadliest murderers.

Produced by David Fincher, who also directed seven out of 19 episodes in the show, Mindhunter has become a cult-favorite in Netflix' vast library. The show is moody and well-made, as expected of a Fincher production, but it's also slower-paced than the usual binge-worthy Netflix offerings, making it only a modest success. Anchored by the unlikely yet pleasant chemistry between Groff and McCallany, the show is fascinating for creating thrills out of conversations instead of the conventional crime spectacle. Unfortunately, because of its disappointing performance by the platform's standards, the show ended in its second season. As Fincher has exited Netflix, a continuation is most likely not happening.

In Money Heist or La Casa de Papel, a group of criminals is recruited by the Professor (Alvaro Morte) to carry out an almost impossible heist on the Royal Mint of Spain. The following seasons also see the group, with more additions to the team, back in action targeting the Bank of Spain's gold reserve to secure their lives away from the authorities once and for all. The ensemble cast includes Ursula Corbero, Itziar Ituño, Pedro Alonso, and Najwa Nimri.

With a total of five parts, Money Heist is Netflix's first international hit. The first two parts of the show are actually a re-edited version of the series' original run on a local TV channel in Spain. The striking imagery of Dali masks and red jumpsuits combined with incredible melodrama and intense action make it an immediate audience favorite. Parts three to five were produced by Netflix on a significantly larger budget and they paid off without losing the essence of the previous episodes. The performances from the ensemble cast are so engaging and memorable, resulting in many of the cast starring in high-profile projects afterwards. The series has spawned a Korean remake and a prequel spin-off focusing on Pedro Alonso's Berlin.

Arcane follows sisters Vi (voiced by Hailee Steinfeld) and Powder (Ella Purnell), who become entangled in the tensions in the prosperous city of Piltover and the struggling undercity of Zaun. The sisters eventually find themselves on opposite sides of an escalating conflict involving a new technology, with Powder transforming into the unpredictable Jinx and Vi attempting to reconnect with the person she once knew.

Before 2021, if someone said a show based on the popular game League of Legends is good, we would probably laugh it off. The joke's on us because Arcane is currently the highest-rated Netflix show on IMDb. The show boasts a stunning animation, inventive visual style, and exceptional voicework. The central relationship between the main characters drives the series and makes audiences invested in following their journey until the end. The themes that the show tackle are surprisingly complex and in-depth, examining class divisions and power. Arcane is not only one of Netflix' best shows, but also one of the finest video game adaptations of all time.

Four Netflix characters, four completely different ways of showing up for somebody. This is the one who fits how you actually do friendship, and what having them in your life would be like.

You do not do friendship at half volume, and neither does he. What you want is somebody who says yes to the plan before you have finished describing the plan.

You are the friend who makes things happen, and so is she. Together you would be mildly exhausting to everyone around you and completely delighted with yourselves.

You have no patience for pleasant lies, and she has no ability to tell them. What looks like coldness from the outside is the most reliable friendship on this list.

You look after whoever is having the worst day. So does he. It is the least strategic way to move through the world and the best possible quality in a friend.

The Queen's Gambit follows Beth Harmon (Anya Taylor-Joy), an orphaned chess prodigy who discovers her talent for the game while growing up in an orphanage. After being adopted by a troubled couple, Beth begins competing in chess tournaments, and eventually sets her sights on the international chess scene dominated by Soviet grandmasters. However, Beth also struggles with loneliness and drugs, which hamper her career.

Released during the pandemic lockdown period in 2020, The Queen's Gambit becomes a worldwide phenomenon and even responsible for introducing chess to a brand new generation. Adapted from the book and directed by Scott Frank, the series has a great production design, costumes, and direction. Anya Taylor-Joy shines as Beth who gives a complex performance as the troubled prodigy. She's joined by a strong ensemble cast that includes Moses Ingram, Harry Melling and Bill Camp. With its tightly written story, exceptional performances, elegant presentation, and satisfying character development, The Queen's Gambit is one of Netflix' near-perfect miniseries.

The Haunting of Hill House follows the Crain family, whose lives are changed after spending their childhood in a supposedly haunted mansion where they experienced a series of disturbing supernatural events. Years later, a tragedy brings the family back together and they are forced to confront the memories and traumas they have spent years avoiding. The series stars Victoria Pedretti, Oliver Jackson-Cohen, Henry Thomas, and Carla Gugino.

Created by modern horror master Mike Flanagan, The Haunting of Hill House is not just filled with empty scares. The series is filled with dread that goes beyond jump scares, building tension through unsettling imagery, hidden ghosts, and haunting sound design. It uses horror to explore grief, trauma, and family dysfunction. The series' biggest strength is its emotional storytelling, as each sibling's struggles feel like different responses to the same deeply painful childhood. While every episode is great, a couple of episodes, namely "The Bent-Neck Lady" and "Two Storms", are standouts for their story and technical achievements. Flanagan went on to create more exceptional horror shows, but this remains his best masterpiece.

The Crown chronicles the reign of Queen Elizabeth II, beginning with her marriage to Prince Philip and her unexpected ascension to the throne. Across six seasons, the series explores the royal family's personal relationships and key events that shape the Queen's standing as monarch, from the Suez Crisis to the whole ordeal with Princess Diana. The Queen is played by Claire Foy, Olivia Colman, and Imelda Staunton, with each of them taking the role for two seasons.

The Crown is one of Netflix's best and most fascinating shows because of its ability to turn historical events and political developments into intimate, character-driven drama. Series creator Peter Morgan is no stranger to royal family drama, having written The Queen with Helen Mirren years before. Its outstanding performances by seasoned actors and newcomers, particularly across the show's cast transitions, allow the characters to evolve naturally across decades. It is all further elevated by impeccable production design, costumes, and cinematography that make every season feel remarkably immersive.

Dark begins with the disappearance of a young boy in a small German town, which then brings four families into a generational mystery that spans decades. As the residents search for answers, they uncover a network of secrets involving time travel and the town's nuclear power plant. The story initially is set in 2019, but then jumps backwards and forwards to many timelines. The series stars Louis Hoffman, Lisa Vicari and Oliver Masucci, among many in the ensemble cast.

A convoluted story is often considered a drawback in films and series. However, for Dark, the more complicated the story becomes, the more intriguing and captivating it is. The sprawling, complicated premise created by Baran bo Odar and Jantje Friese is turned into a compelling, emotionally driven story. Its intricate timeline, intergenerational relationships, and constantly shifting revelations demand attention, but the series never relies on easy answers. The series trusts the audience completely to piece together the puzzle. Its gloomy atmosphere, haunting score, and meticulous storytelling become a staple of this show. Few science-fiction series are as ambitious in their construction, and even fewer manage to make a mystery as good as Dark.

A family saga with a supernatural twist, set in a German town where the disappearance of two young children exposes the relationships among four families.

‘Ramayana’ Language App Cinedubs Named IFFI Accessibility Partner After Reaching More Than 100,000 Moviegoers in 42 Countries

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The mobile app that let more than 100,000 moviegoers in 42 countries hear “Ramayana” in a language of their choosing has signed on as accessibility partner for the International Film Festival of India (IFFI) 2026 in Goa.

The platform, Cinedubs, has brought its audio description and closed-caption technology to the festival, so that visually impaired and hearing-impaired audiences can watch films in theaters alongside everyone else. Related Stories Agatha Christie Series 'Tommy & Tuppence' Coming to Channel 5 in the U.K.

Nitesh Tiwari’s epic “Ramayana,” starring Ranbir Kapoor as Rama, Sai Pallavi as Sita and Yash as Ravana, opened worldwide on Nov. 5. Cinedubs allowed viewers to pick their own language no matter which version their local cinema was playing, and the company said that service added close to INR40 million ($425,000) to the film’s collections.

The system works through the Cinedubs app. Users download a soundtrack in the language they want and listen through headphones, while the app automatically locks the audio to the film on screen. The same technology carries audio description for visually impaired viewers and closed captions for hearing-impaired viewers.

“Being chosen as the Accessibility Partner for IFFI 2026 is a moment of immense pride for the entire Cinedubs team,” said Aditya Kashyap, founder of Cinedubs. “We firmly believe that cinema is a universal language and that neither linguistic barriers nor disabilities should prevent anyone from experiencing the power of storytelling.”

The company pitches its service to filmmakers, producers and distributors as a way to reach audiences they couldn’t previously get to and to earn extra revenue in the process.

Kashyap said the “Ramayana” numbers validated the company’s goal of making cinema borderless. “Accessibility is not just a social responsibility; it is also a significant opportunity for the global film industry to expand its audience base and unlock new revenue streams,” he said.

During IFFI, Cinedubs plans to meet with filmmakers, production houses, distributors and exhibitors about building a film business that is both more accessible and commercially viable. The company also sees a role in pushing Indian films further into international markets.

Built by Dubswork Mobile Pvt. Ltd., Cinedubs can be downloaded from both Apple’s and Google’s app stores.

For cinemas, the app’s multilingual soundtracks run without any changes to existing projection or sound systems.

West Ham have slipped down to 12th place in the Championship table following their 1-0 loss away at Portsmouth on Tuesday night. WHUFC are now three points off the top eight behind Swansea City.

The Hammers turned to Scott Parker as their head coach earlier this month after their decision to part ways with Nuno Espirito Santo. However, the ex-Fulham, AFC Bournemouth and Burnley man has struggled to make an impact so far and is yet to win in his first four games at the helm.

Brazil, who played for the likes of Ipswich Town, Tottenham Hotspur and QPR during his playing days, has delivered his verdict on the London outfit on talkSPORT: “It’s only four games (for Parker). I’m sure he can turn that around but defensively they are giving so many easy goals away. They’ve got to stop that.”

He added: “Listen, I still think they’ll be hovering top six (eight). I was hoping that they’d have a great start. I like West Ham but it hasn’t to be.”

West Ham are back in action this weekend as they aim to return to winning ways. They take on high flying Stoke City at the London Stadium.

West Ham haven’t kept a clean sheet in their last six matches. As Brazil has alluded to, WHUFC have been leaking too many goals.

The Hammers need to tighten up their defensive department if they are to turn their fortunes around over the coming weeks. They will get the chance to bring in some new faces when the January transfer window opens up for business.

Parker will be eager to put his own stamp on his newly inherited squad this winter. He knows what it takes to get out of the second tier having guided Fulham, AFC Bournemouth and Burnley up in the past.

He is under pressure already at West Ham though, despite only being four games into his spell. If they lose again against Stoke City on Saturday, the heat will be further intensified.

West Ham are still the top scorers in the division. WHUFC have scored 38 goals in their 17 fixtures.

The Hammers are always a threat going forward and if they can bolster their backline, there is no reason why they can’t rise back up the league. They managed to keep hold of Jarrod Bowen in the summer and he has found the net on nine occasions this term.

Parker will be disappointed with his start as manager but the players are still getting used to his style. He won’t get patience from the supporters though and needs wins fast.

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Capcom has announced that its underrated multiplayer dinosaur shooter Exoprimal is going to go completely offline on September 29, 2027.

As everyone knows, Capcom is easily one of the best developers in the business right now, and has been ever since Resident Evil 7 successfully revitalised its main franchise. Since then, it’s been hit after hit from Capcom on every level, from new entries in beloved series like Onimusha to completely new games like Pragmata.

Capcom has been doing so well lately that it’s actually easier to point out the games that haven’t been successes. Really, there’s only one that comes to mind, 2023’s multiplayer dinosaur shooter Exoprimal, which still saw decent reviews and an initially passionate community that sadly aren’t enough to save it from being taken offline in less than a year.

Earlier today, the official Exoprimal Twitter account announced out of nowhere that the game is going to be taken offline on September 29, 2027, less than a year from now. A post on the game’s website confirms the official timing and what exactly the “end of service” means for Exoprimal going forward.

It's taken more than a decade, but Capcom has finally made good on Resident Evil 6.

“We cannot thank you enough for the love and support you have shown over the years, and we sincerely apologize for the gravity of this announcement.”

Since yesterday, Survival Pass Premium Tiers have been removed from stores, which will be followed by all DLC getting taken down on March 30. Then the end of service later in the year means the game will “no longer be playable” and won’t even be downloadable from the store once it’s taken offline.

Essentially, Exoprimal is being straight up deleted from the world next September. While the low player counts suggest this has been a long time coming, fans don’t seem happy with how far things are being taken, with some requesting a free-to-play or offline version instead, especially since Exoprimal was actually a good game with plenty to it that just didn’t reach the audience it needed.

LATAM Refused My Refund Within 24 Hours, And The Reason Makes No Sense

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Over the weekend, I booked an expensive ticket for my dad, brother, and me, from Buenos Aires (EZE) to Santiago (SCL), only to come across a better deal after I’d booked. I called customer service to cancel within the 24-hour cooling-off period, but was refused a refund for the most convoluted and unnecessary reason.

LATAM is the airline I’ve flown with the second most in my life after British Airways, my old home airline. It’s an airline I look forward to flying with for several reasons. However, this recent booking experience has uncovered a gaping hole in its customer service, which in the air I’ve always found to be excellent.

This whole situation could have been avoided if I’d been able to pay with my UK credit card on LATAM’s Brazilian website.

The Brazilian site has no issue when entering UK credit card details.

However, the default setting for postcodes (ZIP codes) is numerical-only because, as in the US, Brazil’s postcodes consist only of numbers. In the UK, they’re made up of letters and numbers, so I couldn’t add the postcode that my credit card is registered to.

I tried using only the numbers of my postcode (the one in the example above is random), but the site returned an error, saying it failed to connect to my bank. My guess would be this wouldn’t be a problem for US credit cards, given that ZIP codes consist only of numbers too.

I remember this happening previously, but I never had an issue with a booking that I couldn’t change online.

Ideally, I would have paid with a Brazilian bank account or credit card, but I’m still working through the immigration bureaucracy required to open one.

First of all, I tried to cancel by logging into my LATAM Pass account, but was redirected to the contact center. This is when I realized how much of an obstacle my booking via the UK website would be.

After being put on hold 3 times for what seemed like forever, the agent I spoke with at LATAM’s Brazilian call center told me there was nothing she could do to help me.

She explained the only way I’d get a refund was to call the UK call center, since I’d paid in GBP on the UK website. I called just before the cut-off of the 24-hour cooling-off period, so by then I had exceeded it. I had only left it so close to the cooling-off period because I was researching the trip again the following day and found a better deal; otherwise, I wouldn’t have wanted to cancel.

If I had called sooner and had more time, I could have asked someone in the UK to call for me, as my Brazilian phone number can’t make outgoing international calls. I did top up my UK SIM Card with 20 GBP, but at a charge of 2 GBP per minute, the call disconnected after 10 minutes on hold with no answer.

Irrespective of whether I could have found an easier way to contact the UK call center, I can’t help but feel that my issue should have been resolved there and then over the phone by the Brazilian call center agent. After all, I booked directly through the airline’s website, and then called the airline directly to cancel. It makes no logical sense!

Because of a stupidly convoluted system, I’m stuck with a LATAM ticket that I can no longer cancel, even though I called customer service within the 24-hour cooling-off period.

It’s not the end of the world, as we can still take the flights we’d originally booked, but it’s so frustrating to be put in a situation like this in 2026 because of such an archaic technicality.

I’m still a big fan of LATAM itself, and will continue to fly it, at least until my Platinum status runs out next year.

What’s the most frustrating post-sale customer service experience you’ve had?

Women Filmmakers From Kyrgyzstan, Romania and Mexico Push Past Tokenism at IFFI Goa

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Filmmakers from Kyrgyzstan, Romania and Mexico said they want to be recognized for artistic merit rather than token representation, speaking on a panel about women in world cinema at the 57th International Film Festival of India (IFFI) in Goa.

Titled “Her Lens, Her Legacy, Her Liberty: Women Shaping World Cinema” and moderated by Indian filmmaker Abhishek Jain, the session brought together Kyrgyz writer-director Narghiza Dotieva, Romanian Roma actress, playwright and filmmaker Alina Șerban and Mexican American writer-director Suzanne Andrews Correa. All three urged emerging filmmakers to stay rooted in their cultural and emotional experiences, arguing that authentic local stories can travel. Related Stories Agatha Christie Series 'Tommy & Tuppence' Coming to Channel 5 in the U.K.

Dotieva, whose feature debut “Skylark” explores borderland histories, traced her interest in storytelling to her grandfather’s recitations of “Manas,” the Kyrgyz epic poem. She came to independent filmmaking from advertising and draws on her country’s cultural heritage. She also spoke about the importance of audiences and festivals supporting stories from underrepresented regions.

Andrews Correa spoke about the decade-long journey of making her debut feature “La Cazadora” (“The Huntress”), which premiered in the World Cinema Dramatic Competition at the Sundance Film Festival in January. Set in Ciudad Juárez and starring Adriana Paz (“Emilia Pérez”), the film draws on real-life cases of feminicide and on the story of “Diana, the Huntress of Bus Drivers,” the vigilante behind the killings of bus drivers in the city in 2013. Andrews Correa won the Short Film Jury Award at Sundance in 2019 for “Green.”

Șerban, whose directorial feature debut “I Matter” explores Roma identity, faced discrimination growing up in Bucharest. She has used theater and cinema to challenge stereotypes and reclaim Roma narratives, and recalled funding rejections because her scripts did not fit conventional portrayals of the community.

Șerban also described her presence at IFFI as an emotional return to an ancestral homeland, pointing to the historical links between the Roma community and Rajasthan in northwest India.

Is there an NFL game tonight? Thanksgiving Eve matchup, Week 12 schedule

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

NFL fans everywhere are already dreaming about the league's marquee, three-game slate for Thanksgiving, but this year, they won't have to wait until the holiday starts for Week 12 action to kick off.

For the first time in NFL history, the league will be host what it is referring to as a "Thanksgiving Eve" game. The Los Angeles Rams will host the Green Bay Packers in a contest that will mark the beginning of what figures to be an exciting race to the 2026 NFL playoffs.

The Thanksgiving Eve contest will kickstart the NFL's 16-game Week 12 slate, which will mark the first week without byes since Week 4. It will also be the beginning of a three-day stretch featuring five games – one on Thanksgiving Eve, three on Thanksgiving and one on Black Friday – as the NFL continues to expand upon its slate of late-season, stand-alone games.

Here's everything to know about the NFL's Week 12 schedule, including how to watch the first Thanksgiving Eve game.

Yes, the NFL has scheduled a game for Wednesday, Nov. 25, marking the first time the league has played a game on the day before Thanksgiving.

The Los Angeles Rams are set to the host the Green Bay Packers in a contest that could prove vital in determining the outcome of the NFC wild-card race.

Both the Rams and the Packers will enter play on Thanksgiving Eve sporting 6-4 records. The Rams are tied with the Seattle Seahawks for second place in the NFC West while the Packers are in last place in the loaded NFC North.

Green Bay, Los Angeles and Seattle are also tied in the race for the NFC's No. 7 seed. The Packers have the inside track to earning that playoff berth, so it will be important for the Rams to do what they can to flip that in their head-to-head battle with Matt LaFleur's squad.

The Packers-Rams game will be broadcast on Netflix, which owns the rights to the league's first-ever Thanksgiving Eve game. The contest will begin at 8 p.m. ET – 5 p.m. local time in Los Angeles – and will kickstart the biggest holiday week on the NFL's calendar for 2026.

Healthcare affordability won the midterms. Now employers need to speak up.

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Employers should care about the debate over Affordable Care Act (ACA) subsidies for a simple reason: They pay for the uninsured whether or not those people ever join their health plans.

When people lose coverage, they don’t stop getting sick. They may delay treatment, skip medications or eventually show up in emergency rooms with conditions that could have been managed earlier. The costs don’t disappear, and employers are already struggling with what they pay for healthcare.

The midterm elections made clear that Americans are fed up with rising costs. According to NBC News’ exit polling, 85% of voters expressed concern about healthcare costs, and Congress is now under pressure to respond. Much of the conversation has focused on the expiration of enhanced ACA subsidies but employers have been largely absent from the debate, despite providing coverage to more Americans under 65 than any other source.

At Collective Health, we administer health benefits for self-insured employers covering roughly 700,000 people. Our claims data shows employer healthcare costs are on track to increase at least 8% if not more in 2026. While that’s an improvement from the trend we saw across the nation in 2025, it’s still an 8% increase on an already unsustainably expensive baseline.

As a physician, I’ve ordered thousands of tests and treatments. For most routine blood tests, the needles, tubes, machines and results are essentially the same regardless of where the blood is drawn. Yet in our data, employers pay roughly six times more when those tests are performed in hospital outpatient departments rather than independent labs or physician offices.

The same pattern holds elsewhere. Surgery costs approximately three times as much in hospital outpatient departments as it does at more affordable sites of care, while infusions cost nearly three times as much.

And it’s not as though people are suddenly using significantly more healthcare. Among employers with comparable data across 2025 and 2026, hospital admissions were flat or down during the first seven months of this year. Overall utilization declined for many of our larger employers.

There are areas where we want to see more care being used. Outpatient behavioral health is one example. Helping people access treatment earlier can keep them healthier and reduce the need for more intensive care later. But paying several times more for the same lab test or infusion because of who owns the facility does nothing to improve someone’s health.

Now consider what happens when millions of Americans face higher insurance premiums or lose coverage altogether.

Others won’t have an employer plan to fall back on. And the reductions to Medicaid funding raise another set of concerns. Medicaid is a major source of funding for long-term care, skilled nursing and home-based services, which are already under pressure from staffing shortages and rising demand. As more people lose coverage, hospitals may look to offset uncompensated care costs by charging commercially insured patients more, putting additional pressure on employers already struggling with rising healthcare costs.

Employers have good reason to care about the stability of both markets. The individual market provides an important source of coverage for part-time workers, early retirees and employees’ families. When that coverage becomes harder to afford, the consequences reach well beyond the people buying insurance on the exchanges.

Congress should start with policies that address what care actually costs. Site-neutral payments are an obvious place to begin. If a patient receives the same treatment in a hospital-owned outpatient department or an independent facility, there is little justification for the enormous price difference we see today.

We also need better transparency around prescription drug pricing, pharmacy benefit managers and drugs administered in hospitals and clinics, where markups can be particularly difficult to see. Employers shouldn’t need a dedicated data team to understand what they’re paying for.

The No Surprises Act’s independent dispute resolution process also deserves another look. While the law has helped protect patients from certain unexpected bills, the payment dispute process has created opportunities for inflated demands. And Congress needs to provide a stable individual insurance market, whether by restoring enhanced subsidies or developing a better alternative.

Employers have options of their own, too. One of our clients redesigned its surgical benefits so eligible procedures at a designated center of excellence were free to employees. The same procedures carried 10% coinsurance at an ambulatory surgery center and 20% at a hospital outpatient facility.

In the first year, the share of eligible surgeries performed in hospital outpatient settings fell by nine percentage points. Other services that weren’t included in the redesign saw no comparable shift.

The employer didn’t achieve that simply by changing the plan. Employees received information during enrollment, targeted communications and personalized guidance from Collective Health when they began seeking care. They weren’t left to figure out the options on their own.

That’s the work employers should be doing with their benefits partners. They can manage where infusions happen, not just which drugs are covered. They can reach people before a procedure is scheduled rather than after the bill arrives.

In this industry, we tend to talk about “members,” but these are patients trying to do what’s best for their health while making ends meet. Asking someone dealing with knee pain or a serious diagnosis to research hospital prices and navigate complicated insurance rules on their own isn’t reasonable. If we want people to choose more affordable care, we have to make that choice easier.

Employers have spent years absorbing healthcare cost increases, often with little control over the prices behind them. They have the purchasing power and the claims data to push for something better. With a new Congress preparing to take up healthcare affordability, they also have an opportunity to influence the policies that determine what they’ll pay in the years ahead.

The opinions expressed in Fortune.com commentary pieces are solely the views of their authors and do not necessarily reflect the opinions and beliefs of Fortune.

Dr. Ari Hoffman is SVP of product and chief clinical officer at Collective Health, a technology-enabled third-party administrator (TPA) that helps self-insured employers manage healthcare benefits, control costs, and improve the member experience. Previously, he was an associate professor of clinical medicine at the University of California, San Francisco (UCSF), where he held several clinical leadership roles. He earned his medical degree from UCSF.

Mark Cousins Backs Kyrgyzstan-Set Refugee Drama ‘A Requiem in Bishkek,’ a FIDMarseille Prize Winner (EXCLUSIVE)

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Mark Cousins, the Northern Irish filmmaker behind “The Story of Film: An Odyssey,” is executive producer on “A Requiem in Bishkek,” the first feature from Indian director Di Sica Ray, which took the Claudia Cardinale Foundation Award at FIDMarseille.

The India-France co-production world premiered in the French festival’s First Film Competition. The prize rewards emerging filmmakers and backs debut features that push boundaries. Related Stories Agatha Christie Series 'Tommy & Tuppence' Coming to Channel 5 in the U.K.

Played in English and Russian, the film centers on a South Asian refugee in Kyrgyzstan who makes experimental work from discarded 16mm film stock. He lives alone in a mountain garage belonging to a Pakistani businessman and picks up spare cash looking after a cricket farm run by a Chinese man. His efforts to interest a local producer in his screenplay stall because of his refugee status.

Walks through a cityscape of Soviet-style buildings bring racist abuse that reopens old wounds. His one sanctuary is the city museum, where he finds calm among the paintings of Isaac Levitan.

When rioting erupts, he loses his job and everyone he leaned on. Shattered, he throws himself into cameraless animation, and the abstract images he projects onto his wall draw him into a mystical realm that becomes his last refuge.

Cousins tells Variety that a cut was sent to him and he agreed to lend his name as executive producer, a role he described in this case as being its advocate on the festival circuit.

“I loved its tenderness, solitude and somnambulance, and how these relate to its politics of migration,” Cousins said. “I was lucky enough to know Mani Kaul, and I think he would have loved ‘A Requiem in Bishkek.’ As would Sergei Paradjanov I think. It is part of a very rich seam in cinema.”

Kaul was the Indian modernist behind “Uski Roti,” while Paradjanov directed the Soviet-era classic “The Color of Pomegranates.”

Marilyn Brakhage, widow of American avant-garde filmmaker Stan Brakhage, called the film moving, sorrowful and tragic, and said that at a time of crises and conflict around the world, finding greater empathy for people adrift in a hostile world matters deeply.

“At the same time, it is so profoundly beautiful in its evocation of the restorative powers of Art, and of the world of Art as being that place, the only place, ‘that truly welcomes my soul’ as the character says,” she said. “… But as Stan would have said, ‘It’s not just about our relationship to the Earth, but our connection to the stars.’ He would have been deeply honored by your remembrance of him.”

Ray has spent the past decade moving between Russia, Kyrgyzstan and other ex-Soviet states, working as a freelance photojournalist. He has previously made shorts in Kyrgyz, English, Japanese and Italian, and he edited “A Requiem in Bishkek” himself.

“I believe my art can help relieve humanity of its obsession with hatred towards fellow human beings,” Ray said in his director’s statement.

Thanikachalam S.A. produced the film, with Carine Peyrieres as associate producer. Mirlan shot it, and Peter Theremin composed the music, with Ray’s treatment describing a score that draws on Tchaikovsky. Rana Eid handled sound design, Lama Sawaya was re-recording mixer and Moaz Elgabry graded the picture.

The director participated in the WAVES Film Bazaar, the market component of the International Film Festival of India in Goa.

Ray’s next projects will tackle political, anthropological and ethnographic subjects, often folding in magical realism and folklore drawn from Asian storytelling traditions, in keeping with the Film for People movement he follows.

Orient Express Sailing Yachts Review: Corinthian’s Suites, Dining, Amenities, Service, And More

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

For the main event of my Orient Express adventure via Africa trip, Ford and I had the chance to spend four nights on the Corinthian, which is the first ship in Orient Express’ new cruising venture, known as Orient Express Sailing Yachts.

Orient Express Sailing Yachts set sail in the summer of 2026, and with just 54 suites that can accommodate up to 110 guests, it really brings luxury cruising to a new level. It’s a fascinating time for the luxury cruising industry more broadly, given how competition has been heating up.

I also recently reviewed Ritz-Carlton Yacht Collection, and had a great experience, and reviewed Four Seasons Yachts, which is definitely more luxurious, but also has some growing pains. Lastly, I’ve compared all the experiences as well.

While Orient Express Sailing Yachts is the highest priced cruising venture to date, I have to say, the experience really is unbelievably good, and at least you feel like you’re getting an amazing experience. I come into these kinds of trips with a critical eye, and I was just blown away by the ship’s design, the quality of the food and drinks, and the service. To me the ship felt like a step back in time (in terms of its classic elegance), while also being cutting edge modern.

If you can swing this experience, I’d highly recommend it. Okay, enough of an intro, here comes my 8,500-word review (yikes, this is the longest review I’ve ever published of anything).

Orient Express Sailing Yachts charges per suite (rather than per person, based on double occupancy), and you’ll find that journeys typically cost an average of $4,000-6,000 per cabin per night for a base suite. Make no mistake, this is the very top end of the cruise market, and at this point you’re paying safari prices for the experience.

Let me also emphasize that rates go up from there, and Orient Express Sailing Yachts has some of the most insane suites you’ll find at sea, some of which can cost six figures per sailing. Currently, Orient Express offers cruises in Europe in summer, and in the Caribbean in winter, with transatlantic sailings in the spring and fall.

For what it’s worth, you’ll find rate parity regardless of which booking channel you reserve through. So your best bet is to book through a travel advisor affiliated with Accor Preferred, because they can add perks to your journey. The size of the credit depends on the accommodation type booked, plus the length of the journey.

In the interest of full disclosure, Ford was invited on this Orient Express Sailing Yachts journey (due to the amount of volume he books), and I tagged along with him. All opinions expressed are my own, and I plan to review this exactly the same way I reviewed the two previous cruises (which we paid full price for). I haven’t been asked to write anything, and I have no incentive to be anything other than honest. If people are going to drop a ton of money on a luxury cruise, I want to make sure they’re at least getting the best experience possible.

We experienced Orient Express Sailing Yachts in early October 2026, shortly before the ship’s transatlantic sailing. Admittedly it wasn’t the most exciting itinerary, but that’s just what worked out for us in terms of timing, since we wanted to minimize our time away from home, had to schedule it around when family could best be with our kids, etc. Below is the itinerary for the sailing we took.

We were happy with this itinerary, since our primary goal was to see the ship. It definitely wasn’t the most exciting, though, given that we docked at the port in Mahón (rather than tendering), and it also meant we didn’t have a marina day (not that you’d necessarily want that in October, since it’s a bit chillier). But the point is that if you’re traveling more in peak season, you can expect a marina day more consistently.

Historically, cruising has been all about offering an all-inclusive experience. That’s true with Ritz-Carlton Yacht Collection, but then Four Seasons Yachts countered the trend, by only including breakfast, and then charging extra for lunch, dinner, and drinks (I don’t love this concept, especially given the quality issues they have).

While Orient Express Sailing Yachts might charge a lot, what I really appreciate is how the company has an ultra all-inclusive concept, to an extent that’s even greater than you’ll find on most other cruise lines. Orient Express Sailing Yachts journeys include the following:

Orient Express Sailing Yachts journeys don’t include the following:

Regarding the Orient Express Sailing Yachts tipping policy, the company states that gratuities are included in the fare — “however, if you wish to offer a tip as an extra gesture of appreciation, it will be warmly received.”

I just have to emphasize that the level of inclusions really impressed me. Seriously, I was served caviar with breakfast, lunch, and dinner at various points (and even cocktails!), and the house champagne is Veuve Clicquot La Grande Dame 2018 (which retails for around $200 per bottle).

Orient Express Sailing Yachts currently has a single ship, which is named the Corinthian (a second ship is being introduced in 2027, and it’ll be named the Olympian).

The ship entered service in 2026, and is 722 feet (220 meters) long, 82 feet (25 meters) wide, and 26,600 gross tons. With 54 accommodations and a maximum capacity of around 110 guests, there’s a roughly 1.5:1 staff to guest ratio, given that there are around 170 staff onboard. The spaciousness of this ship, and the amount of space allocated to each passenger, really is next level.

The Corinthian also has the title of being the world’s largest sailing yacht, as it has three automated SolidSail rigid sails on tilting carbon masts, to provide wind propulsion, alongside hybrid LNG auxiliary power. It’s just such an elegant and distinctive looking ship, that it can’t help but turn heads. For those curious, it was explained that the sails reduce emissions by around 10%, and no, the ship doesn’t “heel” in the way a smaller sailboat would. Unless you’re looking up, you wouldn’t realize there are even sails.

You can find the full Corinthian deck plan here. As you’d expect for a sail boat, there aren’t quite as many floors as on a traditional cruise ship. Guests can access a total of five floors, between levels three and seven. To briefly cover that:

Near the ship’s entrance, you’ll find a very cool directory of sorts, which shows the floorplan of the ship. You can click any amenity, and then it will light up, so that’s super handy.

The ship has two sets of elevator banks, as well as two sets of staircases, so getting around the ship is really easy. We always used the stairs, since we were typically within a couple of floors of where we needed to be.

As you’d expect, the ease of boarding a ship varies greatly depending on its size, but Orient Express Sailing Yachts really took it to the next level in terms of how seamless it was. We had spent a night at the Hôtel Dieu Intercontinental Marseille. Our journey included the transfers, so an Orient Express representative met us at the hotel, and then we were driven to the port.

Getting onboard the ship literally couldn’t have been easier. There were no formalities in the port, but instead, we were escorted straight through security, and then check-in was handled once onboard.

There were no other guests as we boarded, and the staff really went above and beyond — for example, the crew member who escorted us through security and onto the ship insisted on holding an umbrella over our heads, to shade us from the sun.

Our check-in process onboard took all of five minutes, and at that point we were introduced to our butler, Reuben, who escorted us to our suite, and took care of everything from there. I should mention how Reuben was German, so they specifically appointed us a German butler, which I thought was super fun (so we communicated with him exclusively in German).

We didn’t have a stop that involved tendering, but for those situations where you do, it’s an efficient process, given how few guests there are. Our one stop had us docked in port, and there was a nice seating area outside the ship with bottled water, sunscreen, etc. Admittedly a setup like this is more useful when you’re waiting on a tender, rather than just walking directly onto or off the ship.

Orient Express Sailing Yachts has 54 suites onboard, spread across over a dozen different categories. The entry level suites here are among the most spacious at sea, while the penthouse suites really take luxury and space to the next level.

Our sailing was rather empty — only 19 of the 54 suites were occupied — and we were upgraded to a higher suite category (I believe they had blocked most of the entry level room categories, as they were doing some minor updates to them ahead of the ship heading to the Caribbean). So let me share my thoughts on that suite first, and then I’ll share some pictures of other suites that I had the opportunity to tour as well.

We stayed in an Appartement Prestige, which is one of the more premium standard suite categories, but below the duplex suites and penthouse suites. These suites are marketed as being an average of 1,119 square feet (104 square meters), so they’re incredibly spacious.

We were assigned suite 518, located on the fifth floor. The hallways on the Corinthian are just stunning, as you can see below. It really feels more like a luxury hotel than a ship, especially in terms of the width of the hallway.

The concept of forgetting that one is on a ship continued inside the suite. The interior felt more to me like a “Palace” hotel in France, than what you’d expect at sea. I can’t even imagine how much money was spent on the interiors, as the quality of the finishes, from the wall treatments to the carpet, as well as the furniture, was just unreal.

Upon entering the suite, there was first a dining area with a table and a chair, as well as a desk with a chair (that chair could also be moved over to the dining table). It’s not often you get this kind of space in a cruise ship suite.

The minibar was also in this area, and was well stocked, with everything being complimentary. This included a Nespresso coffee machine, cocktails, wine, beer, soft drinks, etc. They were also happy to replenish the minibar however you wanted, so there are really no limits.

That entry area then flowed into the living room, which was gorgeous. It had a couch and a coffee table, as well as a hidden TV (which would pop up from in front of the mirror).

Waiting on the dining table was a lovely welcome amenity, consisting of some freshly baked treats, a fruit plate, and a bottle of Veuve Clicquot 2015 Rose. There was also an Orient Express branded journal, along with two Orient Express water bottles, which guests could take home.

Double doors then led into the bedroom, which had a very plush king size bed.

Much like with the bedroom, the bathroom will make you forget that you’re on a ship, as it had double sinks, a soaking tub, a walk-in shower with amazing water pressure, and a toilet in a separate room.

There were also a couple of closet areas, so there was plenty of space to store luggage and clothes.

The suite then had a balcony that stretched the entire length of the suite, with very comfortable furniture.

Beyond the generally beautiful design, it has to be pointed out just how over the top everything was. I mean, the deck furniture is from Loro Piana, just to give you a sense of just how high-end the furniture is.

Housekeeping in the room was great (it was taken care of twice daily, and always well timed), butler service was very useful (more on that a bit later, when I talk about service), Starlink Wi-Fi was lightning fast, and I also love how Orient Express Sailing Yachts includes laundry. You could get unlimited washing, dry cleaning, etc., so that’s a very nice feature.

In the interest of checking out different suite products, I also had the chance to tour the Suite Panoramique, which is the entry level accommodation onboard, located on levels four and five. These are 506 square feet (47 square meters), so they’re spacious for an entry level suite on a cruise.

This had the same general design concept as the suite we were in, except there wasn’t a fully separate living area. The suite had a comfortable king size bed, a desk with a chair, a dining table, and a huge window with a double daybed of sorts.

While there was no bathtub, the bathroom still had double sinks, a walk-in shower, and a toilet in a separate room.

To point out the obvious, these entry level suites don’t have balconies. Of course people prefer to have balconies, but this is just a simple reality of the difference between sailboats and cruise ships, in terms of how many levels there are, how far above water they are, etc.

Orient Express Sailing Yachts’ top end suites are really next level, so I also had the chance to tour the Zephyr Suite, which is located on level seven, and is the second most premium suite onboard. This two bedroom suite has 2,486 square feet (231 square meters) of interior space, plus a terrace that’s 1,206 square feet (112 square meters).

What I thought was so cool was how unique the design of this suite was, as the top suite products seem to have a totally original design. This very much had a residential vibe, with a long hallway connecting the various parts of the suite.

However, perhaps the coolest feature was the replica of an Orient Express Heritage cabin, intended to pay homage to the legendary train.

The Orient Express Sailing Yachts Corinthian has two pools onboard, with one being rather traditional, and one being unlike anything else at sea (at least to my knowledge). The main “leisure” pool is located on the fifth floor, at the back of the ship. While the pool has beautiful aesthetics and lovely deck space around it, this isn’t some destination pool in and of itself. So if a huge pool is a crucial factor in picking a ship, Four Seasons Yachts might be a better option.

We’re all different, and personally, I’m not much of a pool person. Quite to the contrary, I actually loved this pool deck, since it was mostly covered and in the shade, so I could nicely sit there and enjoy the breeze (funny enough, Four Seasons Yachts has an amazing pool, but I didn’t actually spend much time there, due to the lack of shade).

The ship then has a second pool, which is among the most memorable you’ll find on any ship. The lap pool is 54 feet (16 meters) long, and it’s located under the sails. It really feels like you’re in a cave, since it’s on a deck that’s one level down from the main upper deck. What a pool!

On the pool front, really my only disappointment was the lack of a hot tub. As much as I find hot tubs to be kind of gross, I do love them, especially on a ship, so I was sad there wasn’t one of those. In general, the newer ultra high end cruise lines seem to be lacking in that department (the same was true on Four Seasons Yachts).

Beyond the pool setup, on the seventh floor you’ll find a huge deck you can hang out on. There are lots of lounge chairs out here, though it’s not necessarily that popular for just lounging around. The star of the show here is what’s above.

The time when the deck really gets popular is when the ship pulls into or out of ports, given the panoramic views from up here, plus the ability to watch the sails be lowered and raised. I must say, it was absolutely mesmerizing to watch. They made a big event of departures, with live music, cocktails, etc. It really felt like a cocktail reception, only more intimate.

Speaking of deck space, another fun aspect of the ship is how you can actually just freely walk up to the deck in front of the bridge, and even all the way to the bow. How fun!

The Orient Express Sailing Yachts Corinthian wellness complex is located on the fourth floor, and that includes a Guerlain Spa. This area is an impressive 5,380 square feet (500 square meters), with four treatment suites (including a double suite), as well as all kinds of other traditional spa facilities.

Guests can always use the facilities, even without an appointment, and that includes a sauna, steam room, relaxation area, locker rooms, etc.

The spa treatment rooms are absolutely gorgeous, whether we’re talking about the couples room, or the individual ones.

Ford and I got a couples massage during our final day (where we were at sea), and I can honestly say it was one of the best spa treatments I’ve ever had. Not only was the therapist I had incredible, but the massage bed was so comfortable, and there was something so calming about the view, the gentle sway of the ship, etc.

For those curious about the pricing, you can find part of the spa menu below.

The ship also has a full hair salon and barber shop, so there are no shortage of opportunities to get your grooming needs taken care of here. The barber shop even leads to the speakeasy — more on that later.

The Orient Express Sailing Yachts Corinthian has a gym located on the fourth floor, which can be accessed by guests 24/7. The gym isn’t huge, but is more than sufficient given the size of the ship, in my opinion. Every time we used it, we were alone.

The gym had a good selection of cardio and weight equipment, as well as a separate room for aerobics, boxing, etc.

There was an excellent personal trainer onboard, and it was possible to schedule sessions with him at no extra cost. So it’s not just for group fitness, but even for personal sessions, on a space available basis. That’s really nice, as virtually any other cruise would charge for that, in my experience.

The Orient Express Sailing Yachts Corinthian has an incredibly impressive entertainment area, located on deck four. The issue is, it’s not really used a whole lot. Now, I’m not sure I’d actually call this an “issue,” but let me explain.

When you think of mega cruise ships, nightly shows and entertainment are among the most popular attractions. The thing is, the smaller the ship is, the less interest there is in things like this, and that’s especially true for the cruise lines that are primarily targeted at people who haven’t historically been cruisers.

So the Corinthian has a huge theater with dozens of seats, but there’s not a whole lot that goes on there.

Perhaps the more interesting entertainment feature is the private cinema, where you can have them screen a movie (or whatever) for you.

There’s even a recording studio onboard — what a cool concept, and in addition to being able to play instruments here, I heard people have used this to record podcasts, etc.

On the topic of entertainment, the ship also has a card room on the fourth floor, with chess, checkers, decks of cards, etc.

Separately, there’s also the yacht club, which is basically a very elegant lounge area where guests can hang out, watch TV, etc.

Lastly, the ship has the library on the fourth floor, which has an incredible selection of books. Everything on the Corinthian is so thoughtful, including the book selection. As it was explained to us, the 1,500-book library is supposed to represent the most important books of the century, and were curated by the historic Parisian bookshop Galignani.

I know there’s sometimes confusion about whether Orient Express Sailing Yachts is kids friendly, and the answer is… sort of.

On most voyages, guests onboard must be 16 years of age or older. However, on dedicated family voyages, children ages six months to 15 years old are allowed as well. Infants under six months aren’t permitted onboard, and per the terms, “families traveling with little ones under two years old must be accompanied by a dedicated babysitter throughout their voyage.”

Quite honestly, it’s logical that Orient Express Sailing Yachts would have this policy, since the ship’s venues feel really intimate, the pools aren’t huge, etc.

For family voyages, the Corinthian does have a kids club onboard, which offers activities with a dedicated team. Babysitting services can also be arranged for excursions or gala evenings. But in terms of being kids friendly, I definitely think it’s an area where Four Seasons Yachts wins, as all voyages are kids friendly, the pool is huge, and there’s both a kids club and teens club.

Orient Express Sailing Yachts has an incredibly impressive selection of restaurants, bars, and lounges onboard, with the culinary vision overseen by well-known chef Yannick Alléno. Before I get into details, let me just say that I found the quality of food and drinks onboard to be truly exceptional.

I try not to exaggerate, because I think far too many people are a little too generous when describing food on cruises (or flights, for that matter — what’s with all these people describing airplane food as Michelin dining?). However, I found the quality of catering on Orient Express Sailing Yachts to just be in a completely different league than Four Seasons Yachts.

I was also delighted by the generosity of the included options, with an incredible alcohol selection, caviar accompanying dishes at breakfast, lunch, and dinner (and even cocktails), etc. It’s also interesting to note that no reservations are allowed onboard, except at the fine dining restaurant that costs extra.

Everything was always available whenever we wanted it, though people will otherwise probably have mixed feelings on that policy (conversely, the issue when you do allow reservations is that people hog them and then just cancel at the last minute, leaving other guests with limited options).

Anyway, here are the most significant outlets onboard Orient Express Sailing Yachts:

With that out of the way, let’s get into the venues in a bit more detail.

La Terrasse is Orient Express Sailing Yachts’ all-day dining restaurant, serving breakfast, lunch, and dinner. It’s located at the back of the sixth floor, and has both indoor and outdoor seating. The fact that this is the casual restaurant really tells you the quality of the experience, as there are white tablecloths all hours of the day.

This is the primary restaurant where breakfast is served, and you can find that menu below.

Breakfast is entirely a la carte, though at the start of the meal, a basket with a rotating selection of croissants and pastries was brought out.

There was a large selection of fresh juices and coffee available. My one disappointment was that the coffee was just okay, and clearly came from a machine. That’s ironic, because there’s plenty of delicious barista coffee on the ship, so this seems to be one of the only venues where that’s the case. Given the quality of service onboard, I’m sure they’d also be happy to get you a coffee from somewhere else, if you prefer.

We found the breakfast selection to be very good, and over the course of our stay tried the acai bowl with coconut and seasonal fruit, chia pudding with fresh mango, the mixed berry plate, the crispy and soft blinis with caviar cream and smoked salmon, the avocado toast, and more.

We also came to La Terrasse one day for lunch, and you can find that menu below.

We split the chickpea hummus with parmesan to start, and then had the smoked salmon club sandwich and Thai beef salad as our mains. At our server’s insistence, we finished off with a floating island for dessert. YUM.

We also had dinner here our first evening, and you can find that menu below.

To start, we had the smoked salmon with blinis and sour cream, and the gazpacho.

Then for mains, we had the pepper crusted beef fillet with cognac and cream sauce, and the red tuna teriyaki style, along with some sides.

Lastly, we finished off the meal with a selection from the cheese trolley, plus the vanilla millefeuille.

Across the board, the food and service were exceptional, so it’s amazing that this is just the most basic restaurant onboard, as it only gets better from there.

L’Ecrin is Orient Express Sailing Yachts’ elevated dinner restaurant, with a focus on seafood and pasta. It’s located on the sixth floor, and it exclusively has indoor seating. This restaurant doesn’t cost extra, but in my opinion is on par with the paid restaurant on some of the other top ships.

We went here one evening for dinner, and you can find the menu below.

At the start of the meal, we were offered some sinfully delicious bread, along with some cured meats. I’ll talk more about service in a bit, but I might as well just mention in passing how good the crew was about our dietary preferences. For example, we mentioned when we boarded how we don’t eat pork, so without having to say anything, we were brought something that fit our needs.

We took an unusual approach to ordering. To start, we shared the seabass carpaccio with citrus marinade, and the spaghetti with caviar. My gosh, not only was the caviar portion on the spaghetti massive, but the overall flavor of the dish was incredible, as the beurre blanc combined with caviar had quite the unique flavor profile.

Then for mains, we had the warm lobster with olive oil and mayonnaise and the caponata and polenta. Again, this was very, very good.

For dessert, while I had heard amazing things about the suzette pasta (flambeed tableside), we decided on the signature bronte pistachio ice cream coupe, which was insanely indulgent.

What a meal! This was definitely my favorite restaurant onboard.

La Table by Yannick Alléno is Orient Express Sailing Yachts’ most premium dinner restaurant, and it’s the only venue that comes at an extra charge, at the rate of €400 per person for the tasting menu.

First, we have to talk about the restaurant as such. Can you believe this is on a ship? This is without a doubt the most beautiful dining venue I’ve ever seen on a ship. With just over half a dozen tables, it’s also a really intimate space.

The tasting menu is constantly changing, and you can find the menu for our dinner below.

I’ll let the pictures speak for themselves…

The meal was very good, with service that matched the food quality. It really felt like a Michelin-starred dining experience in terms of the presentation, the service flow, etc.

While this was excellent, I will say, I wouldn’t necessarily feel compelled to dine here again. That’s just because the standard restaurants are so great that I don’t necessarily think it’s worth the incremental cost. At a minimum, don’t feel like you’re missing out if you don’t eat here… but definitely do take a look at the restaurant, to appreciate its beauty!

L’Encre is Orient Express Sailing Yachts’ venue that’s exclusively open for lunch, and it was definitely my favorite lunch spot onboard. It’s basically a circular counter with a little over a dozen seats, so the setting is stunning.

You can find the menu below. Think of the concept as basically being like a seafood omakase, where they just keep bringing you out more dishes. Of course you can customize however you want, ask for more of whatever you really like, skip dishes, etc. Below is a sampling of what we ate.

La Piscine Lounge is Orient Express Sailing Yachts’ poolside venue, serving drinks, snacks, and even full meals, throughout most of the day. It’s even opening at night, and can double as a bar for a pre-dinner drink. It’s a beautiful space, definitely not your typical pool bar!

You can find the drink list and menu for that below.

We had an afternoon snack here one day, and split the mezze and the veggie burger, both of which were very good.

Le Bar de la Bibliotheque is Orient Express Sailing Yachts’ coffee shop in the library area, and it’s a lovely place from which to get some work done, read a book, etc.

Here you’ll find barista coffee, homemade cakes, fresh juices, kombucha, and more.

Le Wagon Bar is Orient Express Sailing Yachts’ primary bar venue, and it’s only open late at night. It’s intended to be a replica of the circular bar found on the modern Orient Express trains, with 1920s and 1930s heritage craftsmanship, so it’s a very cool space.

There’s a DJ there at night. The one thing to be aware of is that it only opens really late (9:30PM at the earliest, but often 10PM), and if you’re on a quieter sailing, there might not be too much of a vibe here. However, I’ve heard that on other sailings this place can be packed — it’s all about the crowd on a particular cruise.

I ordered the signature Corinthian Martini, with gin, vodka, champagne cordial, and — naturally, like everything on this ship — caviar!

Next, Le Speakeasy Bar is Orient Express Sailing Yachts’ speakeasy. The idea is basically that people do a bar crawl, and it’s common to move between both bars in the same evening. Much like Le Wagon Bar, this one also opens really late.

The bar is located through a hidden door in the barbershop, so they’re really committed to that speakeasy theme.

This bar even has a separate smoking room, which I’m sure smokers will appreciate (as it’s the only indoor space on the ship where you can smoke).

This bar also has some unique signature cocktails, and you can find that drink list below.

While not included, they are very proud of their liquor selection here, with some bottles that are older than my dad (and at a cost of up to $2,400 per shot — yowzers!).

Tempted as I was, I stuck to one of the included martinis. 😉

Le Flybridge Bar is Orient Express Sailing Yachts’ outdoor bar space located on the top floor. While this is open quite frequently, it really only gets busy when the ship sets sail, given the interest in watching the sails in action.

Orient Express Sailing Yachts of course has 24/7 room service, which is included, with no additional costs. The standard room service menu is on the basic side, but service very much falls into the category of them wanting to make guests happy, so they’re also happy to get you something from any restaurant, if you’d like (but since the menus are always changing, they’re not published in the room service menu).

We did have room service dinner one evening…

…and the final morning we had breakfast in our suite as well (we accidentally ordered too much).

Our amazing butler, Reuben, also gladly brought us cappuccinos and protein shakes, so they were happy to accommodate guests however the could.

Orient Express Sailing Yachts has a very impressive included drink menu. You can find the standard alcoholic drink list below, though some venues have additional options or signature cocktails customized to that venue.

Having Veuve Clicquot La Grande Dame 2018 as the house champagne is very impressive. And let me just say, they’re also not cheap with the pours — you can barely go anywhere without having a glass pushed on you. 😉

Of course there’s also a premium drink list with even more special wines and spirits, and those come at an extra cost.

I have to acknowledge that our sailing was rather empty, so the staff to guest ratio on this sailing was unreal. That being said, it wasn’t just the ratio that was amazing, but the quality of staff was really incredible as well. If you don’t have good staff, they’ll find a way to disappoint even if the staff to guest ratio is amazing.

I’m not exaggerating when I say that service on Orient Express Sailing Yachts was flawless in terms of attention to detail, and that was such a contrast to our Four Seasons Yachts experience.

It’s hard to even pinpoint any one area that impressed me, because it was just very consistent, polished, and proactive, across the board. Let me just give a few examples:

As I said in my Four Seasons Yachts review, while we had an amazing time, we had way more service issues than I’d consider to be acceptable, given the price point. This was exactly the opposite, and it was just flawless service experience after flawless service experience. So while I’m sure service is consistently good, I’m sure the empty sailing also contributed to taking this to the next level.

We were definitely on an odd sailing, in terms of seasonality, since we were on the second to last voyage before the ship sailed across the Atlantic, to start the winter season in the Caribbean.

Our sailing was empty, with just 19 of the 54 suites occupied, so there were fewer than 40 guests. It really felt like having a private yacht, as we rarely saw others, and we’d go to restaurants for dinner, at most a handful of other tables would be occupied.

On our sailing, almost everyone seemed to be a European retiree, so it was maybe in line with what you’d expect from the stereotypical guest profile of Orient Express trains. However, Ford has had some clients on Orient Express Sailing Yachts, and based on the feedback he has gotten, the ship can be an absolute scene, with some pretty over-the-top people.

The guest profile that Orient Express Sailing Yachts gets is probably a bit different than almost any other cruise line. For one, the ship has already been chartered multiple times in its first season, so you can imagine the kinds of people we’re talking about. And even on sailings that haven’t been chartered, there have been some A-list people onboard.

So anyway, we had a very pleasant, understated crowd onboard, but I’ve also heard the crowd can make for quite some people watching, especially for sailings in the South of France, etc.

As I said at the beginning, my goal here is to be as objective as possible, so what feedback do I have? Honestly, as much as I have a critical eye, I think there are shockingly few areas for improvements. Here are the things that stood out to me:

I also want to acknowledge that Trek Trendy had published a review of the Orient Express Corinthian, and I think that’s worth watching for a different perspective. I think he was being totally fair with some of his critique, but I had a different experience:

I travel a ton, and as much as I have so many amazing experiences, I’m almost always excited to go home, given that I miss family, and travel is sort of my “job.” I have to be honest, the Orient Express Corinthian was the first time in recent memory where I thought to myself “man, I wish it weren’t over.”

Earlier this year I experienced Ritz-Carlton Yacht Collection and Four Seasons Yachts, and they’re both great in their own ways. However, Orient Express Sailing Yachts is really in a different league. Yes, it’s priced at the top end of the market, but it also includes the most, and I really appreciate that. There’s something nice about paying a high voyage cost in advance, but then feeling like everything is included.

Orient Express Sailing Yachts really is an unbelievable product, from the design of the ship, to the incredible restaurant and bar concepts, to the service. If you can swing it — and especially if you’ve never taken a cruise before — I can’t recommend this enough.

What’s your take on the Orient Express cruise concept?

3 managers Lincoln City must target after Chris Cohen, Tom Shaw sacking decision

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Lincoln City slipped to within three points of the Championship relegation zone on Tuesday night as they were thumped 4-0 by Charlton Athletic. It made it five consecutive losses for the Imps, and they’ve taken action as a result.

Head coaches Chris Cohen and Tom Shaw have been relieved of their duties, as confirmed on Wednesday morning. The duo served as part of Michael Skubala‘s backroom staff in the League One title-winning season, and stepped into the lead role together after the former boss exited for Bristol City.

Now, after 19 games in charge, the Imps have opted for change. Here, we put forward three contenders the club should consider for their vacant position…

Given that Lincoln City are likely to be in a Championship relegation battle, they could do with someone who has experience in this league. Among the available bosses, few have the level of second-tier pedigree that Paul Heckingbottom has.

The Yorkshireman was let go by Preston North End after a poor start to the season but he has been there, done that and got the T-shirt in the Championship. Not only has he led successful survival fights, but he took Sheffield United to promotion as well.

Heckingbottom’s pragmatic management can make the most of the squad on offer at Sincil Bank. He can lead them to safety before looking to push up the division in the long term.

A similar but perhaps slightly more ambitious option than Heckingbottom is Steve Cooper. The Welshman is on the market after his spell in charge of Danish side Brondby came to an end in May.

Like Heckingbottom, Cooper has led Championship struggles before and led them up the table. His stint at Nottingham Forest was perhaps the best of his career to date, taking them from the lower reaches of the second tier all the way to the Premier League.

The 46-year-old prioritises a stern defensive structure and organisation out of possession, looking to break on counter-attacks with wide threats. His recent Premier League jobs could make this a tough sell for Lincoln City, but they could tempt him into a key role in their exciting project.

Last but not least is a left-field option that some may frown upon, but a move to appoint 63-year-old Antoine Kombouare would be an eye-catching one, and he’s a good fit too.

Kombouare has been managing since 1999 and over the years, he’s led the likes of PSG, RC Lens and FC Nantes. He was most recently in charge of Paris FC earlier this year, and he helped them take 21 points from 11 Ligue 1 games to stave off relegation before resigning.

He might not have a move to the Championship or Lincoln City in mind, but he is an active and successful manager despite his age. He is a no-nonsense boss known for turning around struggling teams with defensive discipline and mental resilience – two key attributes in a relegation fight.

Kombouare may be an unfeasible option, but perhaps he could be tempted to take on a new challenge in England having spent almost his entire managerial career in France.

Wizards of the Coast's War Against MTG Proxies Isn't as Bad as It Sounds

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Wizards of the Coast, a Hasbro subsidiary and the publisher of popular tabletop games Magic: The Gathering and Dungeons & Dragons, has filed a massive lawsuit against a provider of proxy Magic cards, PrintMTG Enterprise, seeking more than $28.6 million in damages.

Proxies are replicas of Magic cards printed to resemble real items. Although they are not allowed in official tournaments, proxies are still somewhat popular in casual play, especially within the Commander format. Notably, Premodern, a format that includes cards from 1995 to 2003 and is steadily growing in popularity, allows proxy cards in some tournaments.

However, while some Magic players are already concerned about a possible visit from the Pinkertons, things aren't as bad as they seem. Wizards is not suing PrintMTG Enterprise for producing proxy Magic cards, but for marketing their products as proxies "to lend them a veneer of legitimacy while actually plying a business built around the sale of counterfeit cards," the lawsuit reads. The distinction is important: "An honest proxy is a stand-in for a real card that makes no claims to authenticity, by appearance or otherwise." On the other hand, "A counterfeit is made to be mistaken for the real card and to take its place as something owned, traded, or sold."

According to the lawsuit, PrintMTG Enterprise operates as "a complex and sophisticated network of individuals and shell corporations," whose purpose is to profit from counterfeiting and trademark and copyright infringement. One of the websites operated by the group, ProxyKing.biz, states: "At Proxy King we try to make our proxies as realistic as possible. This lets you join games and play without people noticing you are running proxy cards in your Magic deck."

Plaintiffs withdrew the lawsuit without a ruling, so it can be revised and refiled at a large date

Magic has expanded quickly in the last few years to become a massive business built on the value of its collectibles. The game is also Hasbro's main revenue driver​​​​​​​; in the third quarter of 2026, Magic surpassed $600 million in quarterly revenue for the first time. In a global trading card industry estimated at around $15 billion, Magic ranks second, behind​​​​​​​ the Pokémon Trading Card Game. While Wizards does not sell single Magic cards, the secondary market still drives the company's product sales. Counterfeits thus hurt the Magic business as a whole, the lawsuit claims. It's not surprising, then, that Wizards isn't going to look the other way when it comes to a counterfeiting network.

However, the lawsuit also makes it clear that the company doesn't have an issue with proxies: "Wizards recognizes that private and noncommercial proxying is a vehicle players use to further engage with the game, not to undermine it." Proxies are important, especially for formats like Vintage or Premodern, where older, expensive cards are very popular. It's understandable that players may not want to sleeve up and shuffle their Black Lotus for a Vintage match at the local game store.

Bitcoin is struggling to recover despite a strong stock market rally, as heavy selling from Bitcoin ETFs continues to weigh on prices. BlackRock’s Bitcoin ETF recorded $1.6 billion in outflows over three trading days, while total November outflows across Bitcoin ETFs reached $4.7 billion, marking the worst month on record. Investors who bought Bitcoin at higher prices are also selling as the price approaches their break-even levels.
