These Are the 6 Luckiest Zodiac Signs This Sagittarius Season

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

From diving deep into the dark waters of your mind to feeling like life is one big adventure park, the switch from Scorpio season to Sagittarius season (November 22 to December 21) can feel like quite a drastic one. Indeed, feel your spirits lift as the cosmos heads toward a much-needed “let’s give life a real shot” sort of energy—just in time for party season. Take a deep breath as the mood lightens, the pace picks up, and possibilities begin to look exciting again. Just remember to keep your feet firmly on the ground as you make the most of everything coming your way, because it can be easy to get swept up in all that excitement.

But while Sagittarius season is usually fun for everyone, this year it’ll hit some signs more than others. Below, see if you’re among the luckiest zodiac signs of the season.

Your season is here, and it means increased luck, serendipity, and cosmic blessings. As the sun moves into your sign, you find that classic Sag fire surfacing once again. You’ll feel more motivated and driven, with that go-getter energy making a comeback. Do you care about the spoils of war that had you weeping last season? Not exactly. Your gaze shifts towards those solid relationships and blessings that keep your life moving in all the stable ways. You are no longer waiting for someone else to show you the way ahead. You are back with a bang, and you know exactly where you are headed.

Ideas: check. Finances: check. Self-confidence: check. Aries, you are thriving this Sagittarius season! Life may have thrown you some lemons lately, but you’ve picked up the pieces and turned them into something creative that excites you, mostly because it pushes you to explore life through a fresh lens. Keep your childlike wonder and belief in your superpowers intact—don’t be afraid to climb that mountain and keep your feet firmly anchored in what sustains you in the long run (the activities and people that truly matter). You could get used to life feeling like a blessing.

You may have found yourself circling back to the same old hiccups: an unavoidable, strained relationship, a lingering lack of self-confidence, a fear of failure… What shifts for you this Sagittarius season is that you decide enough is enough. You take things into your own hands and slowly tend to what you deemed as your unchangeable fate, shaping it into a malleable version of your desired destiny. It will take some practice, patience, and consistent effort. But with your instinctive need for balance guiding you towards steady progress, success becomes predetermined.

You made some strong choices in your recent past. You chose to focus on all that is working for you instead of wallowing in self-pity. You got extremely articulate about your goals and desires. You made peace with the tug-of-war between your heart and mind and found a more purposeful, diligent way forward. This season, you turn that fire up a notch. Keep your eyes locked on your long-term goals, fire your way through with laser-sharp focus, and build and grow with others as you move along. And remember, your relationships are not only a strength, Leo—they’re also a foundation.

You used your season well, Scorpio. You mulled over challenges, ruminated over choices, and tried your best to look at the larger picture. Sagittarius season simply gives wings to all those ideas and shifts you have been plotting silently in your head. You may not be leaping onto the other side just yet, but what you are definitely doing is laying rock-solid foundations for what you are here to build. You’ve set your timelines, fixed your goals, and are now ready to set them into motion.

From conflicting goals to emerging victorious, life has come full circle for you. You stuck to your guns, bit through the challenges, and found your way to the other side. Now that you are here, you feel a renewed sense of energy to build once again, but with greater clarity, sharper vision, and a stronger sense of alignment. Whether it’s in work or relationships, it’s time to play your best hand and give it your all. It’s time to clear the deck and open up to receive. If things feel a bit topsy-turvy along the way, don’t be too quick to read it as a setback.

A Journey Through the Absurd Hell of Trump’s Third-Country Deportations

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The warden kept telling Pheap Rom not to think of himself as a prisoner. Instead, the jailer insisted, Rom and his fellow deportees from the United States were “the king’s guests.”

Here was the warden’s idea of royal hospitality: Each morning at 7:30, Rom—a chiseled, compact, 43-year-old Cambodian man with a pronounced Philly accent—was roused from a bare cell he shared with three other men in Eswatini, a tiny kingdom in Southern Africa. Then he was made to stand at attention as the country’s national anthem, “Nkulunkulu Mnikati Wetibusiso temaSwati,” reverberated through the halls of the Matsapha Correctional Centre. Most meals consisted of a portion of rice splashed with thin broth. It was late 2025, and the weather was warm and wet. For 15 minutes a day, Rom and the other men were allowed to go outside. Before they were locked up for lights out, the anthem played again; thick clouds of mosquitoes kept Rom thrashing all night.

Sometimes Rom would banter with the towering, bespectacled warden, who went by the name Kuenene. If I’m a guest, then why am I being held in a prison with criminals? Rom would ask. Why aren’t I allowed to leave? But Rom knew these exchanges with the warden were absurd, like everything else about his situation.

Before the second Trump administration, the practice of deporting immigrants to countries where they have no ties was a rare and tightly restricted move in US immigration enforcement—reserved mainly for cases where a person simply could not be returned to their nation of origin due to risk of persecution or torture. It has since expanded into a signature White House policy of mass removal. Over the past two years, the administration has inked deals with some 35 nations to accept third-country deportees from the US. The kingdom of Eswatini, which agreed to take up to 160 migrants for a fee of $5.1 million, is one of them. Some of the other destination countries—the Central African Republic, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, South Sudan—are so violent and unstable that they rank on the State Department’s “Do Not Travel” list.

Often making a spectacle of its own transgressiveness, the administration has steamrolled past public outcry, an investigation by Democratic lawmakers, and a warning from the United Nations about a “cascade of human rights violations” resulting from these removals. At least twice in the past two years, US federal courts have ordered the Department of Homeland Security to stop shipping migrants to third countries with insufficient due process. Both times, the Supreme Court has quickly stepped in to let the administration resume.

In September, during one of those brief, court-ordered pauses in removal flights, DHS general counsel James Percival went on X to publish the most comprehensive figures yet on how many migrants have been affected by the practice. He posted a GIF of Taylor Swift mouthing the words “That’s my husband” and pointing excitedly at her wedding ring. Percival’s caption: “How my wife looks at me when I tell her we have deported 25,000 illegal aliens to third nations.”

Rather than slowing down in the face of resistance, in fact, President Trump’s third-country push appears to have inspired imitators. Five European Union countries announced in November that they are working toward a plan to send rejected asylum seekers to a deportation hub in ​Rwanda. In the past two years, Italy has begun sending asylum seekers to Albania. The United Kingdom quietly sent an asylum seeker to Eswatini earlier this year; the man, originally from Sri Lanka, died last month, the government of Eswatini reported. According to local media, the apparent cause was suicide.

Rom’s lawyer, Tin Thanh Nguyen—who is himself the child of refugees from Vietnam—predicts that, if the administration keeps getting away with these expulsions, additional wealthy countries will be emboldened to offload their undesirables to poorer nations for a price. “This is going to be the new model for the West, not only for the United States,” says Nguyen, who also represents three other men currently held in Eswatini and Rwanda. “It’s just kind of out of sight, out of mind for these Western countries.”

But as Rom’s experience reveals, some of these removals may rest on little more than bureaucratic fiction. The US claims it has followed due process with every one of the 25,000 migrants deported to unfamiliar lands. “Their own countries say, ‘We do not want them back,’” US attorney general Todd Blanche told Fox News in early October. Yet in Rom’s case—among countless other Kafkaesque details—his own country never even appears to have been asked.

An American in virtually everything but citizenship status, Rom came to the United States as a 3-year-old refugee from Cambodia. Around 1979, his parents fled their home to escape the horrors of the Khmer Rouge, a genocidal regime that seized power in the wake of Richard Nixon’s carpet-bombing of the country. From there, the family went to a Thai refugee camp—where Rom was born in 1982—and then to a resettlement center in the Philippines. As Rom’s father was selling off possessions to raise money for their ultimate move to America, he was killed by roadside thieves. Rom sat by his side after the attack, his mother later told him, trying to wake his father up.

Rom’s first memories of America are of a cramped, dimly lit bedroom in a small apartment in the Bronx, where he arrived with his mother and six siblings in 1985. “There was just one mattress, and we all slept on that,” he says. “It was a new world to us; everything was new.”

Rom and his family had arrived in the middle of the largest resettlement effort in US history. As America’s chimerical war efforts in Southeast Asia faltered in the 1970s, hundreds of thousands of people began arriving in the US from Vietnam, Laos, and Cambodia. Of the nearly 158,000 Cambodians who gained entry between 1975 and 1994, the majority, like Rom and his family, were refugees deeply marked by the atrocities of the Khmer Rouge.

Around 10,000 of them came to be resettled in areas of the Bronx that were blighted by high crime and poverty. Many were placed in dilapidated housing, with limited job opportunities. Rom’s mother, who didn’t speak English, relied on government support checks and whatever extra income she could make sewing and scavenging. As a child, Rom would help her collect cans and carry the overstuffed bags to the grocery store to return for cash.

With the help of an uncle, the family eventually relocated to Philadelphia, where an adolescent Rom was enticed by a newly formed gang—a Cambodian clique within the Crips called the Blue Dragons. “If you don’t want to get bullied,” older members of the gang told him, “you need to link up with us.” Rom was barely a teenager. An older gang member dubbed him Menace, after Dennis the Menace, because he was “funny and bad at the same time.”

By the time he was 18, Rom had done three stints in juvenile detention and was caught in ever-escalating street beefs. At a New Year’s Eve party in 2008, rival gang members jumped him. Rom waited for a day off from his job—caring for abandoned dogs at a local rescue—to seek revenge. He parked outside the gang’s hangout, and when his targets exited, he opened fire from his car. Rom contemplated running when he heard that the police were looking for him but decided against it largely because he had a young daughter. In jail he would be able to see her, even if it was from behind glass. On the lam, he’d have to disappear. He pleaded guilty in April 2010 to multiple charges, including attempted murder.

Rom was a legal permanent resident of the US, a status he had been granted as a refugee. Though refugees are entitled to obtain full American citizenship, Rom had been told he didn’t stand a chance due to his juvenile record, an obstacle that would greatly alter the trajectory of his life.

Under US immigration law since the 1990s, legal permanent residents who commit crimes that qualify as “aggravated felonies”—an expansive and, critics say, somewhat arbitrary legal category—can lose their immigration status and become subject to deportation after they’ve served out their sentences. In a case of what immigrant advocates call “double punishment,” Rom’s conviction for attempted murder meant he would lose his green card. And he knew that in the post-9/11 era, his home country had bowed to US pressure to accept the repatriation of deported Cambodian refugees.

Rom likes to say that prison saved his life. He spent much of his time in lockup reading, exercising, and making cents an hour working in the kitchen to save up for small gifts for his daughter. As he served out his sentence, a generation of shifts in America’s immigration policy flew by—from President Obama’s support of the Dreamers and record-high deportation numbers to Trump’s policy of family separation to Biden’s border crisis to Trump’s return to the White House. But in Rom’s mind, none of that made a difference for him: His fate was already sealed. He was fully prepared to be deported to Cambodia, a country he had never visited, once his sentence was finished.

Rom’s reserved mood as his prison term neared its end confused fellow inmates. “Yo, why are you not happy?” they asked. “You’re about to be released, you made parole.” On November 13, 2024, Rom walked out after serving 15 years. He was immediately transferred to Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) holding, where what he believed would be a straightforward process turned into a bewildering journey through the American deportation machine.

From detention, Rom did what he could to hasten the process of his own removal. He frequently called the Cambodian embassy in Washington, DC, seeking information on his departure process. He was told that his questions should be directed to the Department of Homeland Security. Officers in Pennsylvania, he says, had no updates. They told Rom they were swamped by the number of cases they were trying to handle. At one point, they told him he would be deported to Thailand, his country of birth, which he told them was incorrect. After six months of detention, Rom filed a writ of habeas corpus arguing that he was being unlawfully detained.

He continued to wait for another three months. On September 24, 2025, his daughter’s birthday, Rom saw on a tablet device that his commissary account had been zeroed out. Among ICE detainees, this is a telltale sign that deportation is imminent. The following day, Rom was transferred to Pine Prairie ICE Processing Center in Louisiana. He was moved again a few days later to Alexandria, Louisiana, where the airport hums with obscure charter airlines that ferry deportees abroad. He waited there for another 72 hours before he started, again, asking officers what was happening.

At first, they said they didn’t know, a claim Rom found hard to believe. When an officer checked his case on the computer system and then quickly clammed up, Rom’s suspicions grew. Finally they told him to pack up, and Rom caught a look at the immigration paperwork. It listed Eswatini as the country of deportation. Confused, Rom turned to another detainee: “Hey, bro, is Eswatini another ICE facility in Louisiana?” he asked. The man responded that it was in southern Africa.

Rom ran to the phone and made a panicked call to his sister in Las Vegas. She searched online and confirmed that Eswatini was indeed in Africa. Rom asked her to call the Cambodian Embassy. Another detainee’s lawyer suggested the group request “reasonable fear” interviews: US Citizenship and Immigration Services conducts these interviews through their asylum officers to ascertain if a person has a reasonable fear of persecution or torture. Immigration agents seemed amused by the suggestion, Rom says. What could the men be afraid of? they asked. “Y’all a bunch of murderers,” Rom recalls them taunting.

Rom pleaded with the guards. Eventually, one officer told him and the others that if they agreed to be shackled, he would take down their request in writing. They stepped into the restraints, and the officer set about filling out some paperwork. As they sat waiting, their feet and hands in chains, Rom gave the request for a “reasonable fear” interview one last try. “Hey,” he asked, “you think you are going to give us that interview?” He realized it was “all fake” and that the officers never intended to file anything.

One by one, under heavy guard, the men were loaded onto a small airplane. When the door closed it was “like my coffin is closing,” says KV, another deportee on the flight who asked to be identified by his initials because he remains detained. The plane flew from Alexandria to Puerto Rico. The officials accompanying the men gave them no information. They tried, and failed, to disable the small TVs that showed the plane’s flight path.

The plane carried on to Senegal, where they were delayed as officials sought permission to travel onward. “They were basically smuggling us through Africa,” Rom says. When the flight touched down at King Mswati III International Airport early on the morning of October 6, the runway was lined with dozens of armed military personnel. Rom was loaded into a paddy wagon. As the van turned onto a dirt road, Rom was overcome with fear. Oh yeah, they are going to kill us, Rom thought. They’re definitely going to kill us.

Eswatini, a country about the size of New Jersey surrounded almost entirely by South Africa, is the continent’s last remaining absolute monarchy. In April 1973, King Sobhuza II suspended the constitution and banned all political parties. The ban remains in place. His son, King Mswati III, took power in 1986, at just 18 years old. Challenges to the monarchy have been met with violence. In June 2021, security forces cracked down on pro-democracy protests, killing 46 people, including two children, according to a government inquiry. Civil society organizations say this is an undercount. A leading human rights lawyer and pro-democracy activist was assassinated at his home two years later. No one has been held accountable for his death.

Eswatini has the highest HIV prevalence rates in the world. Poverty in the country is pervasive. A 2024 report from the US State Department describes Eswatini as having “significant human rights issues,” including torture, media restrictions, and the worst forms of child labor. The king is reportedly married to a cohort of wives and lives a life divorced from his country’s brutal reality. At an April event to mark his 40 years on the throne, he held an extravagant party at a luxury convention center that included a performance by Grammy-nominated singer Davido.

The men, none of whom had committed any crime in Eswatini, were deposited in a high-security area of the Matsapha Correctional Centre. Rom’s cell sat behind a large metal door with a barred window painted forest green. When the detainees arrived, the cells were dirty. They asked for cleaning products but were told that none were available. Basic hygiene supplies took four months to arrive. In a dirt courtyard outside, the men had access to a row of concrete sinks to hand-wash their clothes during their 15 daily minutes of outdoor time. One detainee was allowed to retrieve the clothes in the evening.

Rom’s 15-year sentence in the US proved to be a useful experience. He fell back into his workout routine from prison, knocking out 500 pushups a day along with burpees and sit-ups. He read books donated to the prison, churning through the Hunger Games and Twilight series. His lawyer sent him a copy of First They Killed My Father, a memoir about surviving the Khmer Rouge. Rom had expected the weather to cool down at night, but there was little reprieve from the heat. He was given heavy blankets, which meant choosing between sweltering or being swarmed by mosquitoes. He asked jailers the same question he had been asking the guards in the US. “How long am I going to be here?” No one had an answer.

Gradually, the men were granted a few freedoms. They were brought to a mobile store so those without phones could buy them using money from their families. On the way back, they were allowed to eat at KFC and buy two beers apiece. These were the first excursions Rom had taken outside of detention facilities in more than 15 years.

In a post on X, a DHS official had said that the men being deported to Eswatini were “depraved monsters,” and those statements had spread in local media. So Rom smiled and waved, saying hello to the people who stopped to gawk.

The men were permitted to make one phone call a week using WhatsApp. The calls were broadcast in a common room; a prison official would sit nearby, scribbling notes. When Rom wanted to discuss something sensitive with his sister, the two would switch to speaking in Khmer. Local lawyers were barred from visiting. Shortly after Rom was deported, his sister saw a post on Facebook that mentioned Tin Thanh Nguyen, the lawyer in North Carolina. She got in touch, and Nguyen agreed to take Rom’s case.

Thanks to a TV in the common room that was set to the news, Rom was well aware of the controversy surrounding his fellow deportees. He watched a report about one fellow Matsapha inmate from Cuba, Roberto Mosquera del Peral, who had arrived earlier and was on a hunger strike. Each morning, when officers checked Mosquera del Peral’s cell, Rom says, they would shout, mockingly asking if he was still alive.

How Rom and the others came to be trapped in Eswatini is still unclear. According to a report from Reuters, the deal was brokered in February 2025 between Caitlin Piper, a diplomat who was leading the US embassy in the capital of Mbabane, and Russell Dlamini, the country’s prime minister, who sought and received approval from the king. Neither Dlamini nor the acting government spokesperson, Thabile Mdluli, responded to requests for comment. In May, a spokesperson for King Mswati told Reuters that “the king embraced the deal as Eswatini’s contribution to world order.”

Piper has since departed Eswatini. She was most recently based at the US Consulate in Erbil, Iraq, according to social media posts from the consulate. The consulate did not respond to requests for comment on her current position.

In November 2025, more than a month after Rom arrived and four months after the first deportees were deposited at the prison, Eswatini’s finance minister confirmed that he had received a $5.1 million payment from the US. He said the money would be funneled toward Eswatini’s National Disaster Management Agency. The country said it would accept 160 deportees as part of the agreement. The State Department eventually posted a copy of the memorandum of understanding signed between the two countries on May 14, 2025, which confirmed these details. The signature of the US official includes a looping C, but no name is printed underneath. Asked for comment, the Eswatini Press Inquiries Team at the embassy directed questions to the Department of State in Washington. The Department of State did not respond to questions regarding who signed the agreement.

This payment disclosure led the men in detention to charge that they had been trafficked. “You are moving people around, and money is exchanged,” says KV. “So what is that?” If Eswatini got all that money, Rom wondered, why was he having to spend his own on basics like a fan for his cell?

The Southern Africa Litigation Centre (SALC), a human rights NGO, joined several other groups in filing a legal challenge against Eswatini in 2025, aiming to declare the deportation agreement unconstitutional, obtain the terms of the agreement, and secure an order stopping further arrivals until the matter was legally resolved. The initial challenge was dismissed. SALC and the other groups filed an appeal to the Supreme Court of Eswatini in March. (Just weeks before her reported meeting with Eswatini’s prime minister to facilitate the deportations, Piper had addressed an SALC workshop, urging participants “to bring injustice to light, to demand the respect of those in power, to advocate for a world in which our most cherished freedoms are secured.” )

Rom’s personal path to Eswatini appears to have come about through a combination of cruelty and bureaucratic ineptitude. Nguyen points out that Cambodia has been accepting deportees for more than two decades and that the country is generally responsive and professional in issuing the requisite travel documents. He says that the Cambodian government moved to begin rectifying the situation within a week of his contacting them in October 2025.

Nguyen believes that the US erroneously thought Rom should be sent to Thailand, the country of his birth, not citizenship, but never bothered to contact government officials from that country or from Cambodia. Chann Rotana, a spokesman for Cambodia’s foreign ministry, says they also do not understand why Rom was sent to Africa. He adds that the US did not contact the country’s embassy in Washington, DC, regarding Rom’s case before he left the country. Rotana described the deportation to Africa as “strange” and said the embassy only became aware of the situation when Rom’s sister got in touch. Asked about Rotana’s comments, the Department of Homeland Security directed questions to the Department of State, which declined to respond.

“There’s no rhyme or reason as to why any of my clients were picked to go to Eswatini versus the clients that I have who are actually in Vietnam, Laos, and Cambodia,” Nguyen says. “There’s nothing that makes them distinguishable.”

After an interview with Cambodian officials, Rom’s case for removal progressed while the other men sat hoping for similar developments. When he was cleared to be sent to Cambodia in early February, he requested that he be escorted by a representative from the UN’s International Organization for Migration as a form of insurance. Rom finally arrived in Cambodia on March 26, becoming the second US deportee freed from Eswatini. (The first, a Jamaican man named Orville Isaac Etoria, was deported to Eswatini in July 2025 and repatriated to Jamaica in September of that year.)

When I met Rom in June, at a restaurant in the Cambodian capital of Phnom Penh, he was dressed in all black. The sleeves of his polo shirt were taut around his thick, tattooed arms. We met within walking distance of S-21, a Khmer Rouge torture and interrogation center that is now a museum dedicated to the genocide that set his family on its path to America. He is contemplative about his journey and speaks softly.

After arriving in Cambodia, Rom enrolled in a creative writing class, hoping to detail his experience. He was taking teacher training courses, hoping to land a job at an international school, and picked up work fixing air conditioners when he could.

The jarring transition to life in Cambodia was made harder when his older sister—who had been his lifeline in detention—died in July. “Im trying to hold it together,” he texted me. In August, Rom sent a string of messages saying he’d gotten a job teaching English to a rambunctious group of third graders.

Once a week, Rom calls the deportees who remain in Africa. There are more now. A few weeks after Rom and I met, Eswatini announced that it had received a fourth batch of 11 deportees. Another group followed. Thirty-two have been forced to the country in total. KV remains imprisoned. “With the holidays around the corner,” he texted in late September, “it feels I’m wasting my life sitting here for no reason.” It is unclear when he will be released.

The fate of all third-country deportees stands to be clarified in the coming months. After at least twice granting the Trump administration temporary stays to keep deporting migrants to third countries, the US Supreme Court will start hearing oral arguments on the ultimate legality of the practice in December.

Shortly after he arrived in Cambodia, Rom noticed that Kuenene, his warden at Matsapha, had added him as a contact on Telegram and WhatsApp. Another Eswatini official requested to connect on Facebook and follow Rom on Instagram. Rom accepted some of the invitations and declined others; he couldn’t tell why they were trying to befriend him. Maybe Kuenene wanted to keep tabs on what Rom might say publicly. “He was one of those guys that tell you what you want to hear, smile at your face, make you believe that he is the most supportive guy in the world, and then he will stab you in the back,” Rom said. “I didn’t trust him.” Before he was released, Rom told the guards and other deportees that he might return to Eswatini to visit as a tourist. In reality, he has no intention of ever returning. One visit as a royal guest was enough.

Damian Lewis Boards Short Film ‘Odessa’ as Executive Producer (Exclusive)

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Harald Swinkels' Oscar-qualified World War II-era drama was shot on 35mm in the Austrian-Italian Dolomites.

Damian Lewis and Darren Strowger (Yesterday the Eye Didn’t Sleep) have joined Harald Swinkels’ (Drawback) live-action short film Odessa as executive producers heading into awards season.

“Odessa is much more than a history lesson, because its message grows more relevant by the day,” said Lewis. “It’s beautifully crafted, and I’m proud to stand behind it.”

Added Strowger: “Damian joining us says everything about what this film is. Together, we’ll champion Odessa all the way.” Related Stories Movies 'The Spiral' Director Paolo Strippoli Talks Oscar-Buzzed Horror Pic: "I Try to Lower Expectations" Business "Alarming" Diversity Backtrack Happening In Film and TV Industry In Canada, Report Finds

Set against the backdrop of World War II, Odessa follows Josef, portrayed by Bastian Beyer (Legacy of Spies), his wife Irene, played by Magdalena Müller (Elisa) and their young son Rolf as they cross the Dolomites in search of safety. An act of kindness offers the family a chance at refuge, but suspicion soon closes in. “As the danger mounts, a revelation forces audiences to reconsider what they have seen and whom they chose to trust,” a synopsis emphasizes.

The film was shot in the Austrian-Italian Dolomites. Swinkels uses the texture of 35mm celluloid and the isolation of the mountains to heighten the tension, staying close to the family as fear and exhaustion color each encounter. “History has taught us, time and time again, that monsters don’t come dressed as monsters,” said Swinkels.

Lewis rose to international prominence as Major Richard Winters in HBO’s Band of Brothers and went on to win an Emmy and a Golden Globe for his performance in Homeland. His acting credits also include Billions, Wolf Hall and A Spy Among Friends, among others. Odessa joins A Spy Among Friends and The Radleys among his executive producing credits.

The film from Swinkels’ Exosphere, produced by Rianne Beenes, also stars Johannes Zeiler (Almania). Odessa screened at the Cleveland International Film Festival and Tribeca Festival and was selected for the Santa Fe International Film Festival. He also produced Beyond Silence, which won awards at Tribeca and Palm Springs and was shortlisted for an Oscar in 2025.

Budget carrier Norse Atlantic falls 77% after warning its finances are ‘critical’ amid soaring fuel costs

📅 2026-11-25  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Norse Atlantic shares plummeted Wednesday after it revealed the company is in a "critical financial situation" as soaring fuel costs amid the Iran war strain budget carriers during the winter season.

The Norwegian low-cost carrier said its situation is dependent on securing financing for its short-term liquidity needs.

The company has had discussions about a potential strategic takeover as well as other options including ACMI contracts, which refer to short-term capacity leasing.

"Norse is also engaged in discussions with selected parties on short term financing, however, there can be no assurances that such discussions will result in a viable solution," it added.

The start of the U.S.-Iran war on Feb. 28 sent jet fuel prices soaring, putting pressure on airlines around the world. Prices are now up nearly 72% to $155 per barrel year-on-year as of the week ending Nov. 20, according to the International Air Transport Association's Jet Fuel Price Monitor.

Norse, which offers cheap long-haul flights between Europe, North America, Africa, and Asia, said in October that it was forced to fly with reduced capacity that month, due to "prolonged high fuel prices."

The company said in August that geopolitical disruptions and higher jet fuel costs hit profitability in the first half of the year. Higher fuel costs, lower aircraft use, and operational disruptions led to an $8.4 million second-quarter loss, measured before interest, taxes, depreciation, amortization, and rent.

EasyJet, another low-cost airline impacted by high fuel costs, reported Wednesday that profit before tax slumped 89% to £71 million ($92.7 million) for the year ending in September 2026. Revenue growth was unable to offset the impact of an 11% increase in fuel costs due to the Middle East conflict, the company said.

Earlier this year, Ryanair's CEO Michael O'Leary told CNBC that other European carriers would struggle if jet fuel prices continued to soar. "I think there will be failures."

"If it continues at $150 a barrel into July, August, September, then you'll see European airlines fail and that, in the medium term, would probably be good for Ryanair's business."

Meanwhile, Ryanair's chief financial officer Neil Sorahan told CNBC in May: "I think we will see some of the weaker carriers who were already struggling before the war possibly go to the wall in the winter."

Several smaller European airlines have stopped flying this winter. Slovakia's AirExplore ceased operations, Ireland's Fly4 entered provisional liquidation, and Spain's AlbaStar suspended flights while seeking creditor protection.

Spain's Plus Ultra has also suspended all flights while it restructures, though it has not confirmed a permanent closure.

MLB rumors: Corey Seager willing to waive his NTC, plus latest on Mets, Cubs, Blue Jays, Guardians, more

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

The uncertain 2026-27 Major League Baseball offseason is fully underway. The ongoing CBA negotiations and the near certainty of an owner-implemented lockout on Dec. 2 loom over all, and it may indeed mean a flurry of trades and free agent signings leading up to that date. That was certainly the case leading up to the last lockout of 2021-22, and this time around we've already seen some leading examples. Just recently, for instance, the New York Mets landed the biggest fish in the pond by signing ace Tarik Skubal to a record contract. Not long after, the Chicago Cubs fortified their rotation with Trevor Rogers and their outfield with Teruaki Sato, and Freddy Peralta is headed to the Baltimore Orioles. More's surely to come before lockout day arrives.

Thanks to that prospect of a hastened pace, offseason rumors are flowing. Speaking of which, we'll be here each day to round up what's out there. You can find Tuesday's supply just below.

The Philadelphia Phillies are open to moving Trea Turner off of shortstop given that he's now a pronounced defensive liability. Speaking of openness, veteran shortstop Corey Seager of the Texas Rangers is willing to waive his no-trade clause to join the Phillies and upgrade the position for them, NBC Sports Philadelphia reports.

Now 32, Seager will always have health and durability concerns, but he remains a plus defensive shortstop who produces well at the plate by positional standards. Signed to a 10-year, $325 million deal prior to the 2022 season (just before the last lockout, in fact), Seager is owed another $155 million through the 2031 season. The Rangers are reportedly looking to shake things up. The Phillies, meantime, are trying to keep their contention window open. It's a fit both ways, at least in theory. Such a move would likely mean a move to the outfield for Turner, who himself has very little trade value thanks to his ongoing decline and big contract.

The Toronto Blue Jays have thus far failed to make a big addition this offseason, but they still have targets on the board. The Jays, after missing out on Tarik Skubal, Freddy Peralta, and Trevor Rogers, have moved on to targets like right-hander Kaima Taira out of Japan and lefty Adrian Morejon, the Athletic reports. Taira, 27, ranked No. 2 on our list of notable international free agents this offseason. Here's part of that write-up:

"Listed at 5-foot-8, Taira gets outlier extension down the mound (close to seven feet despite his height), which creates a low release window. Hitters rarely see the ball come from where Taira releases it. He's not a gimmick pitcher either. Taira sits mid-90s with his four-seam fastball and backs it up with a cutter, sinker, sweeper, slider, changeup, and splitter. The sweeper and slider are distinct pitches, as are the changeup and splitter. The Seibu Lions used Taira as a reliever in 2024 and 2025, then started him in 2026, and he handled the assignment well. The breaking balls are just OK, and the fact of the matter is that pitchers this height don't often hold up under a starter's workload. I would assume the team that signs Taira will give him a chance to start. In the long run, though, he might be a reliever, perhaps even a multi-inning setup man. Taira was officially posted on Nov. 1."

Morejon, meanwhile, is particularly intriguing. He's young for a free agent at age 27, and he's coming off a dominant season in the San Diego Padres' bullpen. Thanks to his elite stuff and full repertoire, he's also a strong candidate to transition to the rotation moving forward, though there's reporting that he'd rather stay in the bullpen. That makes him a coveted presence on the market, and the Jays have the resources and willingness to land him.

As noted above, the Chicago Cubs have been energized on the market lately, but they still have work to do after losing so many contributors to free agency. On that front, the club may be eyeing the aforementioned Taira, who's making the leap from Japan, the Athletic reported before the latest flood of moves.

While Taira's role in MLB is TBD, the Cubs still have vacancies in the rotation for 2027 and beyond.

The Cleveland Guardians have agreed to a free-agent contract with left-hander Kris Bubic, Sportsnet reports. The contract is for $17.5 million over one year, according to ESPN. Bubic, 29, made nine starts for the Kansas City Royals last season, and after the trade to the Los Angeles Dodgers, he was used out of the bullpen. Bubic has an outlier fastball thanks to its rise and his unusual release point, and he's an interesting "ceiling" candidate in the rotation. However, his substantial history of injuries may mean he's best used out of the bullpen.

The Detroit Tigers have added infield depth by agreeing to terms with Ha-Seong Kim on a one-year, $5 million free agent contract, ESPN reports. Kim, 31, is coming off a rough partial season with the Atlanta Braves, but he has a more palatable OPS+ of 91 for his career. He's been a primary shortstop in recent years and also has significant time spent at second base and third base.

Even after achieving a reunion with veteran starting pitcher Sonny Gray, the Minnesota Twins are still looking to fortify their roster for a return to contention in 2027. The club still hopes to make one more significant addition this offseason and add another player who may not grab as many headlines, the Athletic reports. Atop the wishlist are a first baseman or a second baseman and another lefty reliever.

Even after the splash addition of Skubal, the Mets may not be done adding starting pitchers in the service of a rebound season in 2027. On that front, the Mets may be interested in adding a starting pitcher on a short-term deal for next season, the Athletic reports. That would likely limit them to the back half of the market, especially with so many bigger names reaching agreements.

Danaos Corporation (DAC) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Line-Dry Your Laundry – The Average Electric Dryer Costs About $125 a Year

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Hang most of your laundry on a folding rack or a line, since an electric dryer costs about 40 cents a load at average U.S. electricity prices. A $30 rack pays for itself after about 75 loads you hang instead, and you can still keep the dryer for towels, sheets, and rainy weeks.

The average electric dryer uses just over 690 kilowatt-hours of electricity a year, a field study the Northwest Energy Efficiency Alliance published in 2026 found. At the average U.S. residential rate of 18.16 cents per kilowatt-hour in August 2026, according to the U.S. Energy Information Administration, that comes to about $125 a year.

The same study found the average household runs 307 full drying cycles a year, so each load costs about 40 cents to dry, and a load you hang costs nothing. A gas dryer costs less to run than an electric one, so hanging its loads saves less.

At 40 cents a load, a couple who wash seven loads a week and hang five of them save $2 a week, or $104 a year, and cut their drying bill by more than 70%. Their $30 folding rack pays for itself in 15 weeks.

You don’t need a yard. A folding rack stands in a corner, a wall-mounted rack folds flat when it’s empty, and shirts dry on hangers hooked over the shower rod. A retractable line can stretch across a tub or a balcony, but check your lease or homeowners association’s rules for a ban on outdoor clotheslines before you hang one outside.

If you hang towels too, take them down while they’re still a little damp, then soften them with 10 minutes in the dryer. Air-drying also spares your clothes the dryer’s heat, which helps them last longer.

For the loads you still dry, clean the lint screen every time and use the moisture-sensor or auto-dry setting instead of a timer, so the dryer stops once the clothes are dry. Run those loads back to back, too, while the drum still holds the last load’s heat.

Hang workout clothes and other lightweight synthetics first, since they dry faster than towels or jeans. At 40 cents a load, every weekly load you hang instead of drying saves about $21 a year.

U.S. consumer confidence surged to a nearly two-year high after midterms

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

The Conference Board index climbed 11.4 points from October to 103.3 as views of the job market improved

David Paul Morris / Bloomberg via Getty Images

The Conference Board said its Consumer Confidence Index rose 11.4 points to 103.3 in November from 91.9 in October, a level last seen in January 2025.

The Present Situation Index, which reflects consumers' views of current business and labor market conditions, rose 13.7 points to 132.8. The Expectations Index, which reflects their short-term outlook for income, business and the labor market, rose 9.9 points to 83.6. The survey ran from Nov. 1 to Nov. 17.

The reading exceeded the Dow Jones consensus forecast of 92.2, according to CNBC.

"Consumer confidence rallied again in November, potentially due to lower uncertainty following the midterm elections," Fergus McCormick, senior economist for global indicators at the Conference Board, said in a statement. "The gain in confidence was led by stronger perceptions of present and future labor market conditions."

The share of consumers calling jobs "plentiful" rose to 30.8% from 26.2% in October. The share saying jobs were "hard to get" fell to 18.4% from 21.4%. The labor market differential improved 7.6 percentage points to +12.4%. Consumers also planned a small increase in spending on goods and vacations, the Conference Board said.

Confidence rose among all age groups, with Gen Z respondents posting the largest gain. Sentiment improved for Democrats and Independents, but Republicans grew less confident. Republicans still held the highest confidence of any political affiliation.

Consumers' average 12-month inflation expectation declined for a second consecutive month to 5.7%. McCormick said households remained unhappy about steep prices and mounting expenses, though some comments struck a more hopeful note about the broader economy.

Consumer confidence is closely tied to what drivers pay at the pump, according to The Associated Press. Gas prices averaged $3.61 a gallon nationwide on Tuesday, down from $4.30 a month earlier.

The University of Michigan's November consumer survey, which came out last week, found sentiment up 7.3% from October, according to CNBC.

The Conference Board's index ended a streak of three straight monthly declines in October, after hitting its weakest reading since 2014 the month before.

2028 Honda CR-V Is a Fuel-Sipping Hybrid Family SUV Worth Waiting For

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Honda’s bestseller goes hybrid only as it enters its seventh generation and will arrive in 2027 with a fresh design inside and out.

Honda’s bestselling model—and the third-bestselling vehicle in the United States through the third quarter of 2026—is getting a complete makeover. The CR-V will follow the path set forth by the sixth-generation Toyota RAV4, going hybrid only as the CR-V enters its seventh generation for the 2028 model year.

We expect the 2028 CR-V Hybrid to wear a sharp new design, with spy photos showing slim daytime running lights above a series of thin horizontal grilles, while the rear end features a light bar that stretches the full width of the SUV. It will likely also be the first vehicle in the U.S. to wear Honda’s new “H” logo, which was originally introduced for its future EVs but will now be applied to other vehicles in its lineup after Honda hurriedly canceled said EVs earlier this year.

The cabin is also expected to get a significant makeover, with the Avancier—a three-row SUV sold in Asia—providing hints about what to expect. The Avancier skips out on most real buttons and knobs, instead putting nearly every function into a large screen mounted to the dashboard, a dramatic shift from Honda’s current setup that prioritizes physical controls. The Avancier’s interior is wrapped in white upholstery, accented by glowing ambient lighting, and features a squared-off steering wheel. Honda also appears to be switching to a column-mounted gear selector.

See all results for new 2028 Honda CR-V Hybrid for sale near 80122

While Honda was once led by the Accord and Civic sedans, in today’s SUV-obsessed market, the CR-V has become the Japanese automaker’s most important model. Its biggest rival, the RAV4, was overhauled for 2026 and went hybrid only. Now, the CR-V will follow suit to stay competitive in the incredibly crowded compact SUV segment. Hybrid sales of the current-generation CR-V have been strong, accounting for over half of the SUV’s total sales so far this year, making it a perfect choice to launch Honda’s new platform and hybrid powertrain.

The CR-V will arrive on a new platform that Honda promises will be stiffer and lighter than before, taking a modular approach that will lead to greater parts sharing and reducing costs. This platform will presumably be adapted to underpin future versions of the Accord and Civic, along with Acura models such as the upcoming next-generation RDX Hybrid.

Honda will ditch the standard 190-hp 1.5-liter four-cylinder engine for the 2028 CR-V, which will arrive exclusively with the company’s new hybrid powertrain. The fifth generation of Honda’s two-motor hybrid setup will continue to use an Atkinson-cycle four-cylinder, along with a pair of electric motors, but the 2.0-liter engine will be a fresh design, featuring a new cooling system. As before, the electric motors will power the wheels most of the time, but the gas engine can also be engaged to directly drive the wheels.

See all results for new 2028 Honda CR-V Hybrid for sale near 80122

The electric motors are more compact than before, and Honda has developed a new battery too. The company says the new hybrid setup will bring improved power and efficiency, targeting 10 percent better fuel economy than the outgoing hybrid powertrain. The current CR-V Hybrid makes 205 horsepower and is rated at up to 38 mpg combined. We wouldn’t be surprised to see power increase to over 220 hp—the RAV4 makes 226 hp in FWD form and 236 hp with AWD—and fuel economy nudge up to just over 40 mpg.

The CR-V is expected to come with front-wheel drive standard and all-wheel drive optional, although it’s unclear if the latter will see the addition of a third electric motor. Honda will also likely include the S+ Shift feature seen on the Civic Hybrid and Prelude, which simulates a traditional transmission’s gearchanges and allows the driver to manually shift with the steering-wheel paddles.

The CR-V’s chief rival will continue to be the Toyota RAV4. However, the Hyundai Tucson Hybrid and Nissan Rogue Hybrid, which both enter a new generation for 2027, will also be strong competitors. The Jeep Cherokee, Kia Sportage Hybrid, and Subaru Forester Hybrid will be in the mix too, along with nonhybrid compact SUVs like the Volkswagen Tiguan.

The move to only offer a hybrid powertrain could see the CR-V’s entry price climb, which might alienate some buyers. Honda will also need to make sure it can match or exceed the efficiency of rivals like the RAV4 while still providing adequate performance and a spacious, usable cabin, given how closely matched the compact SUV space is these days. Moving away from physical interior controls could also upset the Honda faithful who are used to the company’s simple-to-use buttons.

Production is set to kick off at Honda’s factories in East Liberty, Ohio, and Greensburg, Indiana, in April 2027. Sales should begin shortly after, and with the current CR-V Hybrid starting at $37,580, we expect the next-generation model to come in at around $38,000.

Caleb Miller began blogging about cars at 13 years old, and he realized his dream of writing for a car magazine after graduating from Carnegie Mellon University and joining the Car and Driver team. He loves quirky and obscure autos, aiming to one day own something bizarre like a Nissan S-Cargo, and is an avid motorsports fan.

Porch: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Proprietary property data and capital reserves m position the company to scale underwriting while maintaining disciplined risk selection.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 24, 2026 at 1:24PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Porch Group ›

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Oct. 28, 2026, Porch (PRCH +4.26%) announced accelerating policy growth and raised its full-year financial outlook. The company's expansion of its insurance operations shows how high-margin fee revenue can scale earnings without taking on direct underwriting risk.

Porch generates fee income by managing its reciprocal insurance exchange, a policyholder-owned structure that mutualizes risk. Its insurance services segment delivered a 49% adjusted EBITDA margin, or adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization, while achieving a rule of 80 operational benchmark that combines revenue growth and profit margin percentages.

Porch agreed to acquire Allied Trust, a regional homeowner insurance carrier, to expand distribution into states like Louisiana and Texas. Management plans to apply its home factors data, a proprietary collection of property attributes, to prune risky policies and improve pricing across the acquired portfolio.

To support policy growth, the reciprocal maintains a statutory surplus, the regulatory capital cushion required to write premium. That cushion reached $174 million in the third quarter, supplemented by nonadmitted assets, which are investments like company stock that regulators exclude from statutory surplus until converted.

Policy expansion accelerates across regional markets, and leadership is showing enthusiasm for its momentum. By pairing proprietary risk data with expanding agency relationships, Porch gains distribution density while preserving underwriting discipline. Management is watching agency conversion rates.

As acquired portfolios integrate, cash conversion should support long-term profit targets.

Retail turnarounds are taking hold just in time for the holiday season

📅 2026-11-24  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Once-struggling retailers are entering the holiday quarter with improved performance and sales momentum as turnaround efforts begin to resonate with shoppers.

A handful of earnings reports on Tuesday pointed to a mix of factors behind the strong results, including a resilient consumer, stronger execution and demand for the right products at the right price.

Kohl's reported comparable sales growth of 1.2% for the period ended Oct. 31 and raised its full-year outlook as CEO Michael Bender said customers "have remained resilient" but are "migrating to value with their purchases."

He tied improvement to the company's work on product assortment, store experience and value.

Best Buy also on Tuesday posted its highest comparable sales growth in five years, at 6.1%, which prompted the electronics retailer to raise its full-year outlook.

"We have a tremendous amount of momentum," CEO Jason Bonfig told CNBC in an interview. "The fact that we saw pretty much broad-based growth across almost all of our categories is a lot of momentum that we're very excited about."

The company also credited execution, market share gains and a compelling product cycle alongside growing customer demand.

At Dick's Sporting Goods, quarterly comparable sales rose 2.6% at the core business and 0.5% at Foot Locker. CEO Lauren Hobart said "all income demographics grew" and the company "didn't see trade-down from best to better or better to good."

She similarly pointed to new products and market share gains within the category as contributing to the strong quarter. She also highlighted continued improvement at Foot Locker.

All three companies have been undergoing efforts to boost sales and get the right products on shelves to meet customer demand.

Target, likewise undergoing revitalization efforts, last week reported comparable sales growth of 5.6% in the third quarter, fueled by a 5% increase in comparable traffic. The retailer raised its full-year outlook.

CEO Michael Fiddelke at the time called the traffic gain "a healthy sign that guests are responding to our changes and choosing Target more often."

Even Gap CEO Richard Dickson told CNBC the company has moved from "rebuild mode" to "turnaround mode" at its Old Navy banner, a sign of progress, albeit not a broad-based portfolio recovery.

Michael Baker, a retail analyst at D.A. Davidson, told CNBC he sees both a resilient consumer and company-specific improvements as driving sector strength.

"Retail sales have been more resilient than most think," Baker said. His analysis showed retail sales, excluding autos and gas, rose 5.1% in the third quarter on a non-seasonally adjusted basis — the strongest quarter since the pandemic.

But he cautioned that growth appears to be coming more from transaction size than traffic, with inflation, promotions and higher units per transaction driving up spend.

Dollar Tree, which reported better-than-expected earnings on Tuesday morning, posted a 3.8% increase to traffic, accelerating from 0.4% growth in the prior quarter, suggesting more shoppers may be bargain hunting. Comparable sales at the retailer increased 4.6% during the period.

"Consumers are under pressure, and they are increasingly selective about where and how they spend," said Dollar Tree CEO Michael Creedon on the company's earnings call. "When under pressure, shoppers prioritize value and buy closer to need."

Comparable sales of everyday essentials rose 8.9% at Dollar Tree stores in the third quarter, while sales of nonessential items were essentially flat, the company said.

— CNBC's Laya Neelakandan contributed to this report.

Tottenham Offered Chance To Sign Ivan Toney, De Zerbi Needs Him

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

After signing 10 new players in the summer across all areas of the pitch, you would think that Tottenham Hotspur's squad is in a strong state.

It would be fair to say that there is good depth across the defence and midfield in particular, with Marcos Senesi and Luca Williams-Barnett coming into the starting XI against Ipswich Town on Saturday and producing performances that make them undroppable against Sunderland next time out.

Tottenham have now won three successive Premier League matches for the first time in nearly two years, and they are now in the top half of the table having been rock-bottom earlier in the season.

Spurs are certainly improving but there is still a way to go to get to where they want to be under Roberto De Zerbi, who signed a long-term contract to build a project at the Tottenham Hotspur Stadium.

While the north London outfit have looked strong defensively in recent matches, conceding 11 in 11 top flight matches this term, their goalscoring is more of a concern.

Saturday's double against the Tractor Boys took their tally to 12 in 11, but they need to be better than that if they want to really push on and be in contention for a European spot this term.

De Zerbi has had a striker conundrum all season, having begun the campaign with Richarlison in the centre-forward position, even though the Brazilian had informed during the summer that he wanted to leave the club.

After failing to secure a move away from Spurs, the former Everton attacker remains exiled and on the sidelines, while Dominic Solanke continues to fail to show that he is the consistent goalscorer needed.

De Zerbi has used Omar Marmoush in the striker role too, following his summer move from Manchester City, but the Egyptian international has also failed to fire there, and it's difficult to see exactly where he fits in.

It's becoming apparent that the Italian boss needs extra competition for Solanke, and TEAMtalk report that they have been contacted about the potential availability of Al-Ahli striker Ivan Toney by intermediaries.

If De Zerbi wants a striker who has proven he can put the ball in the back of the net regularly in January, there might not be many better options than the England international.

The report claims that the 30-year-old is weighing up a return to England in the New Year, with intermediaries proposing a loan move featuring an option to buy, if Al-Ahli would be willing to agree to that.

A number of Premier League clubs are said to have been contacted by those intermediaries, including Tottenham, Arsenal, Chelsea and Manchester United.

New Crystal Palace manager Thomas Frank is also keen to be reunited with Toney having worked with him at Brentford previously, and that would probably be the landing spot where he'd be guaranteed the most minutes.

Even though Toney has moved to Saudi Arabia, he has continued to be prolific and he has 16 goals in 17 appearances for Al-Ahli this term.

Such has been his form in Saudi that former Al-Ahli sporting director Rui Pedro labelled him 'one of the best strikers in the world', while his England teammate Declan Rice has also been full of praise for him in the past.

Toney's England call-ups followed on from his performances and numbers for Brentford, and his goalscoring form has continued ever since.

He's kind of like Harry Kane in a way where he's not only an out-and-out striker, he's a playmaker as well - his left and right footed passing, the way he picks out his teammates is a special technique to have.

Toney has proven that he can score regularly in the Premier League, and it will be interesting to see who wins the race to bring him back to the Premier League if it's a move that does become possible in the winter market.

Snyder's Soapbox: Until MLB owners open the books, there's no reason to listen to pleas of poverty

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

Welcome to Snyder's Soapbox! Here, I pontificate about matters related to Major League Baseball on a weekly basis. Some of the topics will be pressing matters, some might seem insignificant in the grand scheme of things, and most will be somewhere in between. The good thing about this website is that it's free, and you are allowed to click away. If you stay, you'll get smarter, though. That's a money-back guarantee. Let's get to it.

The world of Major League Baseball -- which includes fans and media, too -- has something rather heavy hanging over its head this offseason. Obviously. We know all about that pesky CBA expiration on Dec. 1 and we know that the owners are all but certain to lock the players out that night while they push for a salary cap in the name of parity.

People are going to take sides, and I make no apologies for being firmly on the players' side. They are the talent and why we watch the games. No one is buying owners' jerseys and sitting in the ballpark facing the suites.

Another reason I'm not a huge fan, in general, of the owners is that I don't believe we're ever getting the full truth about how much money they make. The Braves are publicly traded and we can see a good breakdown of how they fared in 2025. The short version is that they are swimming in money. We could look at the two recently sold MLB teams for a hint, too. The Angels were sold for $4 billion; Arte Moreno bought the team for $184 million in 2003. The Padres were also sold recently for $3.9 billion. The San Diego media market size ranks ahead of only Kansas City, Cincinnati, Milwaukee and, eventually, Las Vegas among MLB cities.

And, again, sometimes you'll have an owner say the quiet part out loud. Enter Orioles owner David Rubenstein:

"A lot of my friends were buying teams, and I realized they're making money. They're making more money than I'm making in private equity," Rubenstein said last month at a forum with other pro sports owners at the Economic Club of Washington, D.C.

" ... I couldn't figure out why it is that people who are fans are so obsessed with the success of their local team. They're not going to make any more money. They're not going to go to the Super Bowl and get a trophy out of it."

People who cape for the owners: this is what you are defending.

Obviously every owner shouldn't be painted with the same brush. Some of them are legitimately fans. But I'd be willing to wager pretty heavily that a majority of the owners think like Rubenstein here and view the teams simply as vehicles to make as much money as possible.

Do we even need to bother with the suggestion that we shouldn't care about our favorite teams since they don't make us money and we don't get pieces of metal trophies? Sports fandom can be one of the purest and most wholesome things about our society. It brings families and friends together. It gives us emotions we otherwise couldn't even possibly imagine feeling. And I'll say this, too: I've seen my favorite team win the World Series and the absolute last thing on my mind was, "man, I'd sure like a trophy for my own mantle." The memories can't be matched by anything material at all. I would take nothing of real, tangible substance over those memories.

It's maddening that an owner can be so out of touch with the product that he purchased.

Do please hone in on the money comment, though. That's a much bigger deal. We've heard owners of some smaller-market teams cry poor for years and even decades, and the response from the MLBPA every single time is a pretty simple one. I reached out to a source and heard it again: "If any team wants us to believe they are losing money, they can simply open their books."

Yes. The Braves have to and we can see how much of a money machine they are in the 11th-ranked media market (counting the two-team cities twice).

Again, there will be exceptions, but the owners generally just view their teams as virtual ATMs, the output of which would be even higher with artificially decreased payroll because of a salary cap. At least one of them doesn't even understand why we care as much as we do.

Jensen Huang addresses Nvidia employees a day after returning as CEO

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Nvidia stock is down more than 7% in Tuesday trading after jumping more than 30% on Monday's reinstatement

Nvidia $NVDA CEO Jensen Huang spoke to staff on Tuesday at a company-wide meeting held at the Santa Clara, California, headquarters, one day after the board reinstated him, according to Bloomberg. The session was slated for a 9:30 a.m. Pacific Time kickoff.

On Monday, Huang came back to headquarters as employees clapped and cheered for him. Speaking briefly in the lobby, he said to employees that he "will do a better job," according to Bloomberg. Huang has said he intends to improve during his second stint as CEO.

Nvidia stock surged more than 30% on Monday, then gave back over 7% during trading on Tuesday. The company's market value has fallen by nearly $2 trillion since it peaked in October.

Senior leaders held a similar employee gathering last week without Huang and used it to show confidence that the company could thrive without him, according to Bloomberg. The board's decision on Monday surprised most of those leaders.

Nvidia said its board voted unanimously to reinstate Huang after receiving input from senior levels of the U.S. government. The company said it is confident his return poses no national security risk. President Donald Trump said he spoke to board members and "made the deal."

Huang left the job on Nov. 12 after reports of multiple affairs. U.S. officials suspect one of the women of relaying information to China's intelligence services, according to Bloomberg, and Beijing has dismissed the allegation. No charges have been brought against Huang.

Several Republican lawmakers criticized the reinstatement. "No national security risk? Based on what?" wrote Sen. John Cornyn, a Texas Republican.

The chairs of three House committees said Huang and Nvidia General Counsel Tim Teter confirmed they will appear for transcribed interviews on Dec. 1 and Dec. 2. The Senate Banking Committee announced a public hearing with Huang on Dec. 3.

You can expect a similar leap between The Witcher 3 and The Witcher 4 as there is between GTA 5 and GTA 6, CD Projekt Red says

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

CD Projekt Red has said people can expect a similar qualitative and technological leap from The Witcher 3 to The Witcher 4 as has been witnessed between Grand Theft Auto 5 and Grand Theft Auto 6.

CDPR joint-CEO Michał Nowakowski made the remark during a Q3 earnings call this afternoon, during which he and CDPR faced a handful of questions related to GTA 6.

"First of all, obviously this is a call about us, not about Rockstar," Nowakowski began by saying, "but I do understand the connection here. Having said that, I'm not going to make a deep-dive and a full-blown review of GTA 5 versus GTA 6. But of course we did look at the game, we did see the differences.

"We believe there's going to be a comparable leap between The Witcher 3 and The Witcher 4 - I think so, yes. It's been 11 years, we are working on The Witcher 4 to make it significantly better looking and improved in terms of pretty much every possible facet of the game, so yes you should expect us to make a much bigger jump between Witcher 3 and Witcher 4."

Another question was about whether GTA 6 had affected development plans for The Witcher 4 and Cyberpunk 2 - a question well-timed after Cyberpunk 2 creative director Igor Sarzynski joked at the weekend that "Cyberpunk 2 is cancelled" because of how impressive GTA 6 is.

"Is seeing GTA 6 launch making us make the games more expensive?" said Nowakowski. "We have certain ambitions when it comes to Witcher [4] and Cyberpunk 2; they have not changed when it comes to GTA 6. If anything happened as a result of GTA 6, it's [that it's] always great to see games like that because they show you how much cool stuff can be done, and that's for sure an inspiration in many ways for our developers."

Specifically in terms of budget, Nowakowski said nothing had changed, and also echoed a point made earlier this year when he said Witcher 4 and Cyberpunk 2 are "not budget-driven developments".

"I'm not saying we don't care about the budgets: we do, and I'm pretty sure [Rockstar] did as well. But you cannot make games like that having in mind 'this is the fixed number of money I want to spend and that's it, otherwise I'm gone'," he said.

"They're ambition-driven developments and money's important - the budgets are not indefinite, that's not what we're saying. But the philosophy is like I said, and honestly that philosophy has not changed for us because of GTA 6's release; it has just always been like that."

When asked whether the arrival of GTA 6 had impacted sales of The Witcher 3 and Cyberpunk 2077, Nowakowski answered "yes and no". "You do see a certain dip in engagement on the PlayStation and Xbox for sure," he said.

Statistics apparently show a drop of "at least" 25 percent in CDPR's players as they "engaged themselves with GTA 6". "But you do have to remember that a lot of our players are PC-based," Nowakowski pointed out. "GTA has not launched on PC; we have not seen any impact whatsoever on the PC sales or engagement. So it's a bit of a mixed answer.

"And also on the PS5 and Xbox, it's not like our sales came to zero," he added. "Some short impact, yes, on the consoles. Long-term, we do not really see that affecting ourselves in any material way in Q4."

Finally, the questioning turned to marketing, which Rockstar has shown can reach far and wide if you have the brand-power of GTA and some imagination. We've seen prestigious department stores like Selfridges taken over by Rockstar for GTA 6, trailers premiering on Netflix, Miami launch festivals - Rockstar's marketing department has gone all out.

CDPR is no stranger to grabbing headlines with glitzy actor hirings such as Keanu Reeves, but will Rockstar's approach to marketing GTA 6 push it a step further?

"The most important takeaway - I'm not sure if I'd call it a lesson because this is our playbook as well - is that if you want to reach a certain critical mass, you do have to get out of just the core gaming bubble, something we did in the past as well," Nowakowski said. "For sure, GTA 6 went there.

"If the underlying question was if we're going to repeat or copy-paste their playbook: no, for sure not. Our games are different and they require a certain different approach - we just do things differently than they do. But in terms of the ultimate effect, in terms of breaking out to the mass market awareness: this is for sure something we consider pivotal to whatever we're going to do with our marketing campaigns in the future."

CDPR's Q3 financial results were positive. The company made less money than this time last year, but the "fresh momentum" - as Nowakowski put it - provided to The Witcher 3 and Cyberpunk 2077 by the release of the Remastered edition of Witcher 3 and the Edgerunners anime series on Netflix, and associated game update, went a long way.

The Witcher 3 reached its highest concurrent player count on Steam as a result, and Cyberpunk 2077 reached its second highest concurrent player count, which represents serious staying power for both games so long after they were first released. And with Witcher 3 expansion Songs of the Past on track for 2027, and The Witcher 4 for 2028, the future is loaded.

XRP’s Extended Base Meets XLM Bounce From Lows

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Home » Altcoins » XRP’s Extended Base Meets XLM Bounce From Lows

Similarities within XLM & XRP’s previous trading behavior adds another layer to the rebound thesis.

Two recent technical takes have focused on XRP’s multi-year chart pattern and XLM’s bounce from a multi-year low, as both tokens trade well below prior highs. XRP sits near $0.92 while XLM trades around $0.125.

Analyst Maxi highlighted a weekly chart showing XRP breaking out of a multi-year consolidation triangle that stretches back roughly nine years.

The post described a “perfect breakout & back-test” and projected that the length of the base could support a move toward $8–10 or higher before any later utility-driven price discovery.

$XRP about to enter PRICE discovery 😳PERFECT breakout & back-test 🎯 Breaking out of a 9 year consolidation.The longer the base = the higher in space!We are looking at a $8-10+ $XRP, before we then enter true price discovery based on utility. pic.twitter.com/3rpFi9i0Hv

The chart itself marks a large symmetrical triangle with price currently near the lower boundary after earlier 2025 highs.

Such long-term patterns are subject to interpretation, and the projected targets represent the analyst’s view of the structure rather than a confirmed outcome. Still, XRP coin remains down substantially from its 2025 peak of $3.65.

Rudra noted that XLM hit a two-year low near $0.12 over the weekend before recovering to roughly $0.136, a move of about 13% off that low and outperforming Bitcoin and Ethereum on the day.

🚨 BOOM: $XLM repricing has started.Saturday: $0.12. A 2-year low.Today: $0.1361. Up 6.73% in one day.✅ +13% off Saturday's low✅ Ahead of Bitcoin. Ahead of Ethereum.✅ Stellar monthly payments: 10x since January✅ 12.7% of all tokenized real-world assets live on Stellar… pic.twitter.com/N153btw3e3

The post pointed to several network developments, including a reported 10x increase in Stellar monthly payments since January and the claim that 12.7% of all tokenized real-world assets currently sit on Stellar Lumens (XLM).

It also referenced the planned connection of DTCC’s tokenized securities platform to Stellar, with DTC-custodied assets expected on the network in the first half of 2027. Additional items mentioned included an upcoming validator vote on a protocol upgrade.

XLM’s market capitalization remains in the low billions, and the token is still far below its historical high.

Both posts emphasize longer-term structural or adoption arguments against a backdrop of recent weakness. XRP’s triangle analysis focuses on the multi-year base, while the XLM commentary highlights relative short-term strength and institutional tokenization progress.

Neither move has yet established a sustained reversal on higher time-frames.
